PREFACE

This Report for the period ended March 2024 has been prepared for
submission to the Lieutenant Governor under Section 49 of the
Government of Union Territories Act, 1963.

The Report contains significant results of the Compliance Audit of the
Departments of the Government of Union Territory of Puducherry
under the General, Social and Economic (including Revenue) Service
Sectors including Departments of Adi-Dravidar Welfare & Scheduled
Tribes Welfare, Backward Classes & Minorities Welfare, Higher &
Technical Education, Local Administration, Public Works, Revenue &
Disaster Management, Rural Development, Tourism and Transport
Departments.

The instances mentioned in this Report are those, which came to notice
in the course of test audit for the period 2023-24 as well as those which
came to notice in earlier years, but could not be reported in the
previous Audit Reports. Instances relating to the period subsequent to
2023-24 are also included, wherever necessary.

The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.



OVERVIEW


0

R

W

This Report contains three Subject Specific Compliance Audits on
(i) implementation of Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee
Act in the UT of Puducherry, (ii) implementation of Jal Jeevan Mission,
(iii) E-Way bill system under Goods and Services Tax and eight compliance
audit paragraphs.

The overall receipts and expenditure of Union Territory of Puducherry for the
year 2023-24 was % 12,183 crore! and हें 12,224 crore? respectively.

We observed several deficiencies in critical areas, which had adverse impact on
effective functioning of Government Departments. Key audit findings
contained in this report are as under:

Audit noticed that the UT Government did not frame Rules to carry out the
provisions of MGNREG Act, as required in the Act. The baseline survey as per
guidelines was not conducted. No separate Annual Development Plan and
Labour Budget were prepared at the Gram Panchayat/Block/District level.
There were gaps in the projected and actually generated person days and the
Department failed to make projections of person days required so as to give
100 days of employment to all the willing Households. The UT Government
did not release its proportionate share of हैं 3.68 crore towards material
component during 2022-24 and रहें 1.24 crore diverted from material to
administrative component which resulted in reduced execution of material
works and non-creation of durable assets.

The employment generated was much less than the guaranteed 100 days of wage
employment and average person days generated were 16.02, 19.61 and 41.69
respectively in the three blocks during the years 2021-24. Only 82 Households
were given 100 days of employment in all the three blocks.

Major portion of works executed were desilting and deepening of water bodies
resulting in non-creation of durable assets. There were shortfalls in conduct of
inspection of works by District Programme Officer. Social Audit was not
conducted since the inception of the scheme. No grievance redressal
mechanism, such as complaint register, was maintained for resolving
complaints.

(Paragraph 2.1)

Revenue receipts - 10,042 crore; Recovery of Loans and Advances - Nil; Public Debt
receipts - = 1,269 crore and Public Account receipts - हैं 872 crore.

a Revenue expenditure - = 10,066 crore and Capital expenditure (inclusive of Public
Account Disbursements) - = 2,158 crore.

xi


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Implementation of Jal Jeevan

Audit noticed that though all the 1,14,969 households in the two districts of the
UT of Puducherry were provided with Functional Household Tap Connections,
as per the e-Jalsakthi portal, scrutiny of records of 26 sampled villages showed
that 3,459 households out of 24,927 households did not have Functional
Household Tap Connections as of March 2024. The Functional Household Tap
Connections were installed outside houses, near doorsteps, instead of the
recommended delivery points at kitchen, washing/bathing area and toilet.

Delay in submission of proposals and unutilised funds led to receipt of only
% 9.53 crore from Gol during 2019-24, as against allocation of = 55.19 crore.
Out of 92 works approved under JJM, five works were not taken up, 81 works
were completed and six works are pending completion. It was observed that no
water meters were installed in the UTP either at water source (borewells, sumps,
tanks) or households connected with FHTCs. Non-installation of water meters
at both source and delivery point resulted in the absence of measurement of
water supplied, thereby precluding consumption-based billing, which could
have reduced water wastage and encouraged efficient water usage.

Despite groundwater being the sole source of drinking water in the Districts of
Puducherry and Karaikal and even though it is classified as critical, no rainwater
harvesting systems were implemented including in areas facing severe water
shortages in summer.

Under AAP 2022-23, seven water augmentation works were taken up (i.e., one
year before the closure of mission). Delay in according expenditure sanction
coupled with slow progress of works resulted in non-completion of these works
even after three years of approval, thereby defeating the objectives of JJM.

Inadequate coverage of water quality testing was also observed. The percentage
of test conducted across CPs, excepts Kottucherry and Nedungadu, ranged
between 15 per cent and 35 per cent, indicating gaps in monitoring, particularly
at the end user consumption level. Further, test reports revealed instances of
chemical contamination in the supplied water beyond the permissible limits.

In the 10 CPs, 204 OHTs and 120 sumps were available for water storage and
distribution. However, scrutiny of record of nine CPS revealed inadequate
maintenance, as 11 OHTs and sumps were not cleaned even once during
2019-20 to 2023-24, while 135 out of 204 OHTs were cleaned only one to four
times during the period. These lapses indicated non-adherence to CPHEEO
norms and posed potential risks to water quality and public health.

Eighty to hundred per cent beneficiaries in three out of 26 villages surveyed
reported dry connections and non-supply of water.

(Paragraph 2.2)

xii


Overview

E-

The Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) on E-Way Bill System (EWB)
under GST was undertaken to examine whether EWB mechanism was
functioning in protecting revenue interest of the Government and to examine
whether the preventive/enforcement activities of the Department in enforcing
EWB mechanism were efficient and effective.

Audit observed certain deficiencies in the EWB system as it allowed generation
of EWBs by nil filers and non-filers of GST returns, by cancelled taxpayers and
by composition taxpayers making interstate supplies. The system also allowed
generation of multiple EWBs for single invoices, payment of taxes in deviation
of applicable tax rates to the HSN codes declared in EWBs. These deficiencies
had a consequential impact of non/short discharge of tax liability and potential
risk of passing on ineligible ITC. Audit also observed cases where EWBs were
generated but the transactions were not reported in GSTR-1 returns, transactions
were reported in GSTR-1 without generating EWBs, transactions were reported
in GSTR-1 without payment of tax in GSTR-3B, taxpayers availing ITC without
inwards EWBs and movement of prohibited goods through EWBs.

Further, the operational preparedness of the Department for enforcement
functions could be further strengthened and deficiencies relating to
non-utilisation of available resources viz., analytical reports and MIS reports.
Audit also observed that Proper Officers were not following the due process in
assessment and collection of Government dues during interception of vehicles.
Audit also encountered scope limitation due to non-production of documents/
information related to cancelled EWBs, time extended EWBs and EWBs
rejected by the recipient etc. Further, records/data pertaining to details of
booked cases or the related files, patrolling of vehicles, manpower and conduct
of inspection etc., were also not furnished to Audit.

(Paragraph 3.7)

Land acquired at a cost of = 13.46 crore for Indira Gandhi College of Arts and
Science, Puducherry remained unutilised for over 15 years due to absence of
approach road, leaving the objective unfulfilled.

incomplete for the past four and a half years, rendering the expenditure
unfruitful.

CHAPTER I

INTRODUCTION


INTRODUCTION

1.1 About this Report

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG) on
Government of the Union Territory (UT) of Puducherry relates to matters
arising from Compliance Audit of selected programmes and activities of
Government Departments.

The primary purpose of the Report is to bring important results of audit to the
notice of the UT Legislature. Auditing Standards require that the materiality
level for reporting should commensurate with the nature, volume and magnitude
of transactions. The audit observations are expected to enable the Executive to
take corrective action as also to frame policies and directives that will lead to
improved management of the organisations, thus, contributing to better
governance.

Compliance Audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to
expenditure, receipt, assets and liabilities of the audited entities to ascertain
whether the provisions of the Constitution of India, applicable laws, rules,
regulations and various orders and instructions issued by the competent
authorities are being complied with.

This Chapter presents the profile of audited entities, authority for audit besides
the planning and extent of audit and follow-up on the previous Audit Reports.
Chapter II contains audit observations arising out of Compliance Audit of
Government Departments. Chapter III contains the audit observations arising
out of audit of Revenue Receipts and Chapter IV contains findings arising out
of Commercial and Trading Activities of Government.

Profile of

There are 42 Departments in the Union Territory headed by Development
Commissioners/Secretaries, who are assisted by Directors and sub-ordinate
officers. There are 13! Government Companies and 72 Autonomous Bodies.

The entities in UT of Puducherry are audited by the Principal Accountant
General (Audit-II), Tamil Nadu and Puducherry.

' Includes one non-working PSE (PELECON).

Chapter I - Introduction

Author pr audit

The authority for audit by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India is
derived from Article 149 of the Constitution of India and the CAG’s (Duties,
Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. CAG conducts audit of
expenditure and receipts of the Departments in the UT of Puducherry under
Sections 13? and 16° of CAG's (DPC) Act. CAG is the sole auditor in respect
of three Autonomous Bodies, which are audited under Sections 15* and 19(2)*
of CAG's (DPC) Act. In addition, CAG conducts audit of 70 other Autonomous/
Local Bodies, under Section 14° of CAG's (DPC) Act, which are substantially
funded by the Government.

The financial statements of the Government Companies (as defined in
Section 2(45) of the Companies Act, 2013) are audited by Statutory Auditors,
who are appointed by CAG as per the provisions of Section 139(5) or (7) of the
Act. The Statutory Auditors are required to submit a copy of the Audit Report
to CAG, which among other things, include financial statements of the
Company as per Section 143(5) of the Act. These financial statements are
subject to supplementary audit to be conducted by CAG within 60 days from
the date of receipt of the Audit Report under the provisions of Section 143(6) of
the Companies Act, 2013.

9 and conduct of audit

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various Departments,
Corporations and Government Companies based on expenditure incurred,
revenue collected, criticality, complexity of activities, level of delegated
financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns of
stakeholders. Previous audit observations are also considered in this exercise.
Based on this risk assessment, the frequency and extent of audit are decided.
An annual audit plan is formulated to conduct audit on the basis of such risk
assessment.

Audit of (a) all expenditure from the Consolidated Fund of UT having a Legislative
Assembly, (b) all transactions relating to the Contingency Fund and Public Accounts
and (c) all trading, manufacturing, profit and loss accounts, balance sheets and other
subsidiary accounts kept in Government Departments.

Audit of all receipts, which are payable into the Consolidated Fund of UT having a
Legislative Assembly.

Audit of accounts of a body or authority to which grant or loan is given from
Consolidated Fund of UT for any specific purpose.

Audit of the accounts of Corporations (not being companies) established by or under
law made by Parliament.

Audit of all receipts and expenditure of a body/authority substantially financed by
grants or loans from the Consolidated Fund of UT having a Legislative Assembly.



Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports (1२5) containing audit
observations are issued to the Heads of the Department, Corporations and
Companies. The Departments, Corporations and Government Companies are
requested to furnish replies to the audit observations within one month
of receipt of IRs. Whenever replies are received, audit observations are either
settled or further action for compliance is advised. Important audit observations
arising out of these IRs are processed for inclusion in the Audit Report of CAG
of India, which is submitted to the Lieutenant Governor of UT of Puducherry
under Article 151 of the Constitution of India and Section 49 of the
Union Territories Act, 1963.

onse to Audit

1.5.1 Inspection Reports

A review of Inspection Reports (IRs) issued up to 31 March 2024 revealed that
3,364 paragraphs relating to 728 IRs remained outstanding at the end of
September 2024 (Appendix 1.1).

Large pendency of IRs was indicative of the fact that Heads of Offices and
Heads of Departments did not initiate appropriate and adequate action to rectify
the defects, omissions and irregularities pointed out in the IRs.

1.5.2 Audit Paragraphs

Eleven Audit Paragraphs including three Subject Specific Compliance Audits
viz., (i) Implementation of Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment
Guarantee Act in the UT of Puducherry, (ii) Implementation of Jal Jeevan
Mission in the UT of Puducherry, (iii) E-Way Bill System under Goods and
Services Tax (GST) which were forwarded demi-officially to the Secretaries of
the Departments concerned between June 2025 and August 2025 to send their
responses within four weeks. Replies of Heads of Department and the views
expressed by the representatives of the Government during Exit Conferences/
Exit meetings were also considered while finalising the Report.

1.5.3 Recommendations

This Report contains specific recommendations on a number of issues involving
non-observance of the prescribed internal procedure and systems, compliance
with which would help in promoting good governance and better oversight on
implementation of departmental programmes and objectives at large. The UT
Government is requested to take cognizance of these recommendations and take
appropriate action in a time bound manner.

Scope of this Report

This Report of the CAG relates to matters arising from the Audit of
nine Government Departments. Chapter II includes two Subject Specific
Compliance Audits (SSCA) viz., (i) Implementation of Mahatma Gandhi
National Rural Employment Guarantee Act in the UT of Puducherry,
(ii) Implementation of Jal Jeevan Mission in the UT of Puducherry. Chapter III
discusses the trend of revenue receipts and response of the Departments/
Government towards audit and one Subject Specific Compliance Audit on
E-Way Bill System under GST. Chapter IV gives an introduction on Public
Sector Enterprises. Apart from the above, there are also eight Audit paragraphs.
These are discussed in Chapters II and III. The audit observations discussed in
the paragraphs in the chapters are observed from the test-check of records in the
selected offices.



CHAPTER II

COMPLIANCE AUDIT


CHAPTER II

COMPLIANCE AUDIT

This Chapter contains the results of Compliance Audit of various departments
of the Government, their field formations and Autonomous Bodies. Instances
of lapses in the management of resources and deficiencies in observance of the
norms of regularity, propriety and economy are presented in the succeeding
paragraphs.

Subject Specific Compliance Audit on Implementation of
Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee
Act in the UT of Puducherry

ya Be | Introduction

2111 Background of Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment
Guarantee Act

Government of India (Gol) enacted the National Rural Employment Guarantee
Act, 2005, which was subsequently renamed as the Mahatma Gandhi National
Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA) (hereafter referred as the Act)
in October 2009. The mandate of the Act is to enhance the livelihood security
of the households in rural areas of the country by providing at least 100 days of
guaranteed wage employment in every financial year to every household whose
adult members volunteer to do unskilled manual work.

Government of Puducherry notified the Scheme ‘The National Rural
Employment Guarantee Scheme, Puducherry’ in May 2008 (hereafter referred
as UT Scheme) as required under Section 4 of the Act.

There are two Districts in the UT of Puducherry viz., Puducherry and Karaikal
which include four regions viz., Puducherry, Karaikal, Mahe and Yanam. Out
of 12.48 lakh population in the UT (2011 census), 3.95 lakh (31.70 per cent) are
living in the rural areas. Both Puducherry (except urban regions viz., Mahe and
Yanam) and Karaikal Districts were notified under the Act.



Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

211.2 Goals of the Act

Social protection for the most vulnerable people living in rural India by
providing employment opportunities.

Livelihood security for the poor through creation of durable assets,
improved water security, soil conservation and higher land productivity.

Drought-proofing and flood management in rural India.

Empowerment of the socially disadvantaged, especially women,
Scheduled Castes (SCs) and Scheduled Tribes (STs) through the process
of a rights-based legislation.

Strengthening decentralised, participatory planning through
convergence of various anti-poverty and livelihood’s initiatives.

Deepening a democracy at the grassroots by strengthening Panchayati
Raj Institutions

Effecting greater transparency and accountability in governance.

2:12 Audit Framework
21.2.1 Audit Objectives

The objectives of the Subject Specific Compliance Audit on the implementation
of MGNREGA in the UT of Puducherry were to assess whether:

> the planning process and financial management practices were
adequately geared towards achieving the goals of the Act, while
adhering to relevant rules and regulations.

> Employment was adequately generated, leading to social protection and
livelihood security as envisaged.

> durable and useful assets were created and maintained in compliance
with rules and regulations, while ensuring convergence with other
programmes/schemes.

> appropriate and adequate monitoring, social audits and grievance
redressal systems were in place to achieve greater transparency and
accountability.

2.1.2.2 Audit Scope and Methodology

An Entry Conference was held on 03 July 2024 with the Secretary to
Government (Rural Development), Government of Puducherry, wherein the
audit objectives, criteria, scope and methodology of the Subject Specific
Compliance Audit (SSCA) were discussed. The SSCA covered the period
2021-22 to 2023-24. The field audit was carried out from July to
November 2024, through scrutiny of records/documents at the UT, District,
Block (including the Gram Panchayats (GPs)) levels. In this SSCA, the two
Districts ie, Puducherry and Karaikal covering three Blocks  viz.,
Ariyankuppam, Villianur and Karaikal (two Blocks from Puducherry District



Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

and one Block from Karaikal District) and 18 GPs! (six GPs from each selected
Block) were selected for the field audit. Sampling of the GPs was done based
on expenditure incurred using Probability Proportional to Size without
Replacement method.

Out of total 3,770 works taken up during 2021-24 involving money value of
297.09 crore, 180 works (10 works from each of the 18 selected GPs) involving
a money value of = 17.77 crore were selected for Joint Physical Verification.
Besides, a beneficiary survey among 216 registered workers (12 beneficiaries
from each of the 18 selected GPs) were also carried out. The results of
beneficiary survey have been included as findings. An Exit Conference was
held on 15 May 2025 with the Secretary to Government (Rural Development),
Government of Puducherry, wherein the audit findings of the SSCA were
discussed. The Government replies (August 2025) to the report have been
suitably incorporated in the relevant paragraphs.

2.1.2.3 Audit Criteria

Audit Criteria adopted for the SSCA are:

> Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act, 2005,
Operational Guidelines 2013, Amendments thereto and Rules issued

under the Act;
> Annual Master Circulars issued by the Ministry of Rural Development
(MoRD), Gol;
> National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme, Puducherry, 2008; and
> GOs, Circulars and Instructions, Guidelines etc., issued by the Gol and

the UT Government from time to time.
2.1.2.4 Acknowledgement

Audit acknowledges the co-operation extended by the Directorate of Rural
Development Department, District Rural Development Agency, Puducherry,
and all the three Block Development Offices viz., Ariyankuppam, Villianur and
Karaikal during the conduct of this SSCA.

2.1.2.5. Audit Constraints

The methodology adopted to arrive at the budgeted person days and works
proposed could not be examined in Audit as the maintenance of records* was

. Six GPs from each selected Block.

Files relating to preparation of Labour Budgets and its approval by MoRD, preparation
of proposal for release of administrative funds and material funds, employment register
ete.

conclusions have been made mainly based on the replies furnished by the

Department/Blocks.

Chapter II — Compliance Audit

Audit analysed availability of institutional mechanisms and the process of
planning for the implementation of the scheme at the GPs, Blocks and at the
District level and the findings are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.1.4.1 Non-conduct of baseline survey

Para 6.2 of the Guidelines stipulates that a survey of job card holders was to be
mandatorily conducted in every GP to prepare a baseline to assess the quantum
and timing of demand for employment in the GP. The baseline survey was to
be done in 2012-13, so that the surveys for all the GPs can be completed in
2013-14 itself.

Audit observed that baseline survey was not conducted as required. Government
replied (August 2025) that the baseline survey has not been conducted in the UT
due to administrative and resource constraints. It was assured that the matter is
now being actively pursued, and discussions are underway with the National
Institute of Rural Development and Panchayati Raj (NIRDPR), Hyderabad, to
undertake the baseline survey in a comprehensive and scientific manner across
all rural Blocks of the Union Territory.

2.1.4.2 Preparation of Labour Budget/Annual Development Plan

Chapter 3 of the Guidelines describes the steps required for setting up of systems
to accurately record the demand for work by the wage-seekers and to make
prior assessment of the quantum of works likely to be demanded. This matching
of demand and supply of work forming the basis of planning under
the MGNREGA, is to be achieved through the preparation of a
Labour Budget (LB)*.

Further, Paragraph 6.3 of the Guidelines provides that the projects to be taken
up as part of the LB should emerge from an integrated plan for local
development. The Annual Development Plan (ADP) has to be consolidated at
District level, based on inputs from Block level and GP.

When the files and the connected records relating to LB and ADP were
requested, the DRDA did not produce any records and stated that the District
level plans/LB had been prepared, approved and submitted to the Gol in time
for the release of central share.

In the absence of ADP, the Department could not ascertain the actual labour
demand, identify works to meet the estimated labour demand or estimate the
cost of works and wages and benefits expected in terms of employment

generated along with the physical improvements. Thus, the Department failed
to identify the needs and resources of different stakeholders as prescribed in the

Includes (i) the anticipated quantum of demand for work, (ii) the precise timing of the
demand for work and (iii) a plan that outlines the quantum and schedule of works to
be provided to those who demand works.

13


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

guidelines ibid which had resulted in meagre execution of material works in the
GPs and non-creation of any durable assets.

Government replied (August 2025) that approved Shelf of projects at Grama
Sabha along with resolution was uploaded in the NREGASoft portal at Block
level which was consolidated as District as well as State DPC/SPC as proposal
for LB every year.

The reply is not tenable as scheme guidelines mandates preparation of separate
ADPs at GP level by following necessary steps prescribed in the guidelines.
Audit further verified the LB details available in the NREGASoft portal and
found that most of the GPs have not entered the labour projection and work
projection details in the portal in violation of the guidelines.

the data available in the NREGASoft portal and observed variations between
the projected and actually generated Person Days (PDs) during 2021-24 as
discussed below.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Audit scrutinised the financial data provided by the Department and the
observations are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.1.5.2. Non-release of the UT share

Government of India had clearly stated in its fund sanction orders that the
expenditure of material component including wages of skilled and semi-skilled
labour would be shared between the Central and UT in the ratio of 75:25.
Further, the UT Scheme also provided that the UT Government shall release the
proportionate amount to the District Programme Co-ordinator within 15 days
from receipt of Gol funds for operating the District Employment Guarantee
Fund.

It was noticed that no funds towards material component were released by Gol
to UT in 2021-22. As against the release of % 3.73 crore in 2022-23 and
? 7.30 crore in 2023-24 released by Gol, the UT Government did not release its
proportionate share of = 1.24 crore and 2 2.43 crore.

Government replied (August 2025) that due to fiscal constraints and delays in
internal approvals, the matching UT share could not be released in timely
manner.

2E5.3 Funds under administrative component

9 Diversion of funds from material component to administrative
component

Government of India released ₹ 11.03 crore towards material component during
2022-23 and 2023-24, however, expenditure incurred on the material
component was only 4.51 crore. Though Gol had released % 1.63 crore only
towards administrative component during the same period, expenditure incurred
on the same wasz 4.51 crore. Thus, it is evident from the above that funds
intended for material component has been diverted to administrative
component, which resulted in reduced execution of material works.

The Government while accepting the Audit observation (August 2025) stated
that such diversion was not in accordance with the prescribed financial norms
of the scheme. However, steps are being taken to ensure this does not happen
in future.

(ii) Excess expenditure of Administrative Fund by the UT

As per Para 12.5.2 and 12.5.3 of Guidelines, Gol provides up to six per cent of
the total expenditure incurred on the scheme in a Financial Year as
administrative expenses to enable the UT for augmenting human resources and
developing capacity for critical activities. The expenditure should be tracked
and monitored to identify deviation and ensure that the administrative
expenditure remains within six per cent limit.

incurring an excess expenditure to the tune of @ 1.10 crore.

Government replied (August 2025) that the administrative expenditure exceeds
six per cent due to the increased honorarium liabilities of MGNREGA
functionaries.

(iii) Short release of Administrative Fund by the Gol

As the UT is eligible for up to six per cent of the total expenditure as
administrative expenditure, it was eligible for Gol share of = 5.33 crore towards
administrative fund during 2021-24. However, an amount of = 1.64 crore only
was actually received though actual administrative expenditure was more than
six per cent i.e., = 6.43 crore. Further, during 2021-22, no administrative fund
was received from the Gol. The inadequate release of administrative fund from
Gol had led to diversion of funds from other schemes/material accounts towards
administrative fund for making salary payment to the MGNREG scheme staff.

Government replied (August 2025) that frequent requests were sent to Gol for
release of administrative funds.

2.1.5.4 Delay in payment of wages and compensation for the delay in the
payment of wages to the workers

Section 3(3) of the Act mandates that workers are entitled to being paid on a
weekly basis and in any case within a fortnight of the date on which work was
done.



Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Scrutiny of data available in the NREGASoft portal revealed that out of
8.76 lakh transactions involving = 95.43 crore during 2021-24 in all the three
Blocks, 1.70 lakh transactions involving = 22.11 crore were delayed beyond
admissible days.

Government replied (August 2025) that the delay occurred mainly due to late
sanction of wage payments by the Ministry, particularly when the labour budget
ceiling was exceeded. As a result, transactions remained pending for want of
wage funds.

The reply is not acceptable as the delay in payment was noticed in all months.
It was also noticed that the Gol is releasing funds periodically as seen from fund
sanction orders.

Further, Section 29(1) of the Act provides that in case the payment of wages is
not made within fifteen days from the date of closure of the muster roll, the
wage seekers shall be entitled to receive payment of compensation for the delay
at the rate of 0.05 per cent of the unpaid wages per day.

Audit analysed the muster rolls and wage payment records available on the
NREGASoft portal covering 180 selected works in three Blocks and revealed
that out of 1.47 lakh transactions, 0.27 lakh transactions amounting to
% 3.68 crore were disbursed to beneficiaries after the prescribed 15-day period.
The details of delayed wage payment in respect HHs are given in Chart 2.3.

BESS Transactions relating to rejected and pending regeneration

Audit analysed the data in NREGA Soft portal relating to the generation of Fund
Transfer Orders and the status of payments made to the workers in all the
Blocks. During the Audit period, it was found that out of 8,49,971 transactions
processed, of which 7,623 have been rejected. Out of the rejected transactions,
7,457 have been regenerated and the balance of 166 transactions were pending
regeneration amounting to = 1.65 lakh. Thus, it is construed that though the
work was done by the workers, payment was not made to the beneficiaries
resulting in financial hardship.

2.1.5.6 Non-availability of mechanism for payment of unemployment
allowance

As per Para 3.5(i) of the MGNREGA Guidelines, if employment is not provided
within fifteen days of application, the applicant is entitled to daily
unemployment allowance. Further, UT Government must specify the rate of
allowance, frame rules for its payment and make necessary budgetary
provisions as per guidelines.

Audit noticed that UT has not framed any rules or specified the rate for
unemployment allowance since the inception of the scheme resulting in
non-payment of unemployment allowance to eligible beneficiaries.
Additionally, the provision for automatic generation of payment orders, as
required under Para 3.5(iv) was not implemented in the NREGASoft portal.

Audit observed that the physical records such as demand register for the
employment demanded and offered were not maintained in any of the selected
GPs. Audit analysed data downloaded from the NREGASoft portal regarding
employment demand and provision for the 18 selected GPs. The analysis
revealed that 21,861 households had demanded employment during the period
2021-24 but were not provided with the required number of PDs. This resulted
in non-payment of unemployment allowance to the tune of @ 1.97 crore*.

Government replied (August 2025) that a proposal has been submitted to the
Government for allocation of funds under the State Employment Guarantee
Fund (SEGF). Upon receipt of the SEGF funds, a formal mechanism will be
put in place for payment of unemployment allowance.

8 No. of days employment not provided x per day wage rate x 0.25.

19


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

2.1.5.7 Non-deduction of TDS under GST from the payments made for
material procurement under Material Component works -
₹ 2.56 lakh

Section 51 of the CGST Act, read with CGST Rule 66 provides for deduction
of TDS under GST at the rate of two per cent (CGST and SGST - one per cent
each) on payments made to the supplier of taxable goods and/or services, where
the total value of such supply under an individual contract exceeds % 2.50 lakh.

Audit noticed that though all the Blocks had made payments to the vendors to
the tune of = 127.94 lakh in respect of 15 works during 2021-22 to 2023-24,
TDS to the tune of = 2.56 lakh was not deducted while making payments.

Government replied (August 2025) that necessary instructions have been issued
to the contractors who failed to deduct TDS, directing them to remit the TDS
amount at their level along with the applicable fine.

2.1.6 Issue of job cards and Information, Education and
Communication Activities

MGNREGA is a demand driven public wage employment programme where jobs
offered whenever there is a demand for work. As on 25 July 2024, there were
76,548 HHs with 1,14,334 registered workers in the UT of Puducherry. However,
in the sampled 18 GPs, there were 12,013 HHs with 16,609 registered workers.

Audit analysed the data of all the three Blocks in the UT of Puducherry in
respect of conduct of Information, Education and Communication (IEC)
activities, processes of issuance of job cards as prescribed in the guidelines, and
the findings are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.1.6.1 Non-conduct of door-to-door surveys

Para 3.1.1(ii) of the Guidelines provides that a door-to-door survey should be
undertaken by each GP every year to identify eligible HHs which had been
missed out and wish to be registered under the Act.

However, Audit noticed that no such a door-to-door survey was conducted by
the Block officials, since inception of the scheme in 2008.

Government replied (August 2025) that DRDA is in the process of obtaining
detailed cost estimates for conducting door-to-door survey from National
Institute of Rural Development (NIRD), Hyderabad. Further, upon receipt of
these details necessary approval will be sought from the competent authority
and survey will be initiated.

2.1.6.2 Non-conduct of IEC activities under MGNREGA

As per Para 5.4.2 of the Guidelines, the UT should develop an IEC Plan on
MGNREGA with a focus on reaching out to the registered workers as well as

20


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

other groups who could benefit from MGNREGA. The IEC plan should clearly
indicate UT, District, Block and local level activities.

Audit noticed that neither any IEC plans had been prepared nor any activities
were conducted at the UT, District, Block and local level. Due to non-conduct
of IEC activities, the beneficiaries remained unaware of the timelines for
payment of wages and provision of unemployment allowance due to them.

During the beneficiary survey, it was also found that most of the beneficiaries
were unaware about their entitlements and rights as per the Act/scheme
guidelines as stated below:

> 44 (20.37 per cent) beneficiaries were not aware of the entitlement of
their wages (Para 5.4 of the guidelines).

= 40 (18.52 per cent) beneficiaries were not aware of the timelines of
payment of wages (Para 5.4 of the guidelines).

> 11 (5.09 per cent) beneficiaries were not aware that minimum 100 days
of employment was to be provided during a financial year (Para 5.4 of
the guidelines).

= 25 (11.57 per cent) beneficiaries stated that holding of Gram Sabha
meetings was not suitably advertised in the Villages and
21 (9.72 per cent) beneficiaries stated that they had not attended the
Gram Sabha Meetings held (Para 5.4 of the guidelines).

¥ 173 (80.09 per cent) beneficiaries stated that they were not aware about
unemployment allowance and 198 beneficiaries stated that though they
have requested for employment, no dated receipts were provided to them
by the GPs for recording the demand.

Government replied (August 2025) that due to limited administrative funds,
full-scale IEC activities could not be undertaken.

2.1.6.3 Delay in issue of new job cards to the registered persons

Para 3.1.5(i) of the Guidelines provides that if a household is found to be eligible
for registration, the GPs has to issue new job card within a fortnight after the
date of receipt of the application.

However, it was noticed that from April 2021 to July 2024, in the selected
18 GPs, out of 1,364 job cards issued, 404 applicants received job cards with a
delay ranging from one day to 825 days, as depicted in Chart 2.4.

21


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Chart 2.4: Delay in distribution of Job Cards

246

Government replied (August 2025) that the delay in issuing job cards was
primarily due to administrative constraints, shortage of field-level staff, and
periodic technical issues in the MIS portal which affected timely processing and
verification of applications.

2.1.6.4 Periodical updation/revision of job cards not done in the Blocks

As per Para 3.1.5(xiv) of the Guidelines, the GPs will undertake an annual
updating exercise of job cards. Further, Para 3.1.5(xiii) of the guidelines
provides that the job cards shall be valid for a period of five years and will have
provision for the addition/deletion of members eligible to work.

However, Audit noticed that neither annual updation nor revision of job cards
had been carried out by any of the Blocks, as envisaged in the guidelines.

Government replied (August 2025) that a door-to-door survey is proposed to be
conducted in collaboration with NIRDPR.

wage employment every year to willing HHs whose adult members volunteer
to do unskilled manual work. The status of employment generation in the
Blocks during 2021-24 are given in Table 2.6.

seasonal factors affecting the willingness of beneficiaries to participate in the
manual work. Further, Government attributed the fact that timely availability
of funds and delays in approval of LBs have also affected the consistent
generation of work.

The reply is not acceptable as it was found that the demand from the workers
were not recorded properly by the GP and Employment not provided as
demanded. Further, the results of beneficiary survey also attributed need for
more employment.

It could be seen from Table 2.7, a total of 8,36,344 PDs were demanded by

Audit observed that in the UT, mechanism for automatic registration of demand
such as kiosks, Interactive Voice Response System and mobile/telephone-
enabled registrations had not been established (November 2024). Further, dated
receipt for work applications were not issued as required under Box No. 7
and 8 of the Guidelines.

Despite the legal guarantee of employment provided under the Act, a significant
number of HHs either received employment or provided with fewer PDs. This
resulted in a substantial loss of 2.76 lakh PDs, and thereby depriving rural HHs
of timely and adequate wage employment.

2.1.7.4 GPs with Nil employment generated

In the Ariyankuppam Block, out of 39 GPs, in respect of six GPs employment
was not provided to the 2,869 registered HHs in 2021-22. Similarly, in the
Villianur Block, out of 42 GPs, 16 GPs had not provided employment to the
5,097 registered HHs in 2021-22, 2022-23 and 2023-24 respectively.

Government replied (August 2025) that out of 108 GPs in the UT, 93 have Gram
Rojgar Sahayak (GRS) in position, while 15 posts are currently vacant. Efforts
are underway to fill these vacancies to strengthen implementation at the
GP level.

2.1.7.5 Shortfall in employment provided to differently abled persons.

Para 9.3.9 of the Guidelines provides that there should be a special drive to
identify all the persons with disability and other vulnerable persons and provide
100 days of work to each of the HH that they belong to in all the villages within
a specified timeframe.

However, it was noticed that no special drive had been conducted to identify the
persons with disability and other vulnerable persons within the villages in any
of the Blocks. Further, out of 2,848 registered differently abled persons, only
1,381 persons (48.49 per cent) with 38,355 PDs were provided employment
during the period 2021-22 to 2023-24 as detailed in the Appendix 2.2.

2.1.8 Execution of Works

One of the objectives of MGNREGA is to create durable assets to strengthen
the livelihood resource base of the rural poor. The discrepancies noticed in
respect of the execution of works and creation of durable assets in the UT of
Puducherry are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.1.8.1 Deficiencies in formulation of Schedule of Rates for MGNREGA
works

As per the provisions of the Act (vide para 17 and 18 of Schedule-1), the UT
Government shall link the wages, without any gender bias, with the quantity of
work done and paid according to the rural schedule of rates fixed after
conducting time and motion studies for different types of work and different
seasons and revisions periodically. A separate Schedule of Rates (SoRs) shall
be finalised for women, the elderly, people with disabilities and people with
debilitating ailments so to improve their participation through productive work.

Audit observed that a separate rural SoRs for three items viz., Jungle Clearance,
Earthwork and Extra lift for the year 2021-22 were formulated based on the
Work Time and Motion Studies (WT&MS) report furnished (February 2022)
by the Puducherry Technological University (PTU), which was followed during

26


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

2022-23 for preparation of estimates under the scheme and for all other
works/items, Puducherry SoRs had generally been followed. The DRD had
requested (December 2023) PTU to revise the SoRs for the year
2023-24. However, the PTU has not responded so far (June 2025).

It was observed that adoption of the Puducherry SoRs for MGNREGA works
had resulted in preparation of work estimates at lesser project costs. An analysis
of MGNREGA work based on estimate prepared with PSR and Rural SoR is
given in Table 2.8.

Table 2.8: Work ID-2332/Bahour East GP/Ariyankuppam Block

It could be seen from Table 2.8 that, had the Rural SoRs been formulated, the
estimate costs worked out and sanctioned cost would have been higher and
consequently would have resulted in generation of more PDs in each of the
works executed.

It was also noticed that no separate SoRs were finalised for women, elderly
people and persons with disabilities to improve their participation, as prescribed
in the guidelines. The non-creation of separate SoRs for women, elderly people,
etc., had resulted in preparation of work estimates at lesser project costs and
eventually in under payment of wages as the actual working efficiency of these
privileged categories of people would be lesser than the normal male workers.

2.1.8.2 Delay in completion of works

Audit observed that a major number of works, which were originally proposed
in the annual plans for which Work IDs had been generated were not actually
executed. Further, out of the executed works in the three Blocks during
2021-24, several works are still in progress without completion.

Audit observed that out of 3,770 works for which work IDs were generated,
1,849 works were completed which included nine ineligible’ works. Of the

° Installation of memorial plaques incurring an expenditure of = 8.09 lakh.

27


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

remaining 1,921 works, 1,754 works were not taken up and 167 works were
ongoing (% 13.88 crore). Further, 54 works relating to 2021-22 and 2022-23 on
which an expenditure of = 1.01 crore had been incurred, remained incomplete
as of November 2024.

Further, though 1,849 works were completed, Project Completion Reports
(PCR) were not recorded in the Works Register in any of the Blocks during
2021-24 as mandated in the guidelines.

The failure to execute the approved and sanctioned works reflects deficiencies
in planning, monitoring and implementation at the Block level which resulted
in loss of PDs to the UT. Urgent remedial measures are required to prevent
recurrence and to ensure optimal utilisation of sanctioned resources.

Government replied (August 2025) that steps are being taken to improve
demand forecasting, ensure timely approvals and fund flow, and align work
execution with seasonal work availability to avoid such delays in the future.

one of the goals and objective of the Scheme. On scrutiny of the data available
in the NREGASoft portal related to the category-wise works executed at the
GPs level in all the three Blocks revealed the following shortcomings in the
execution of different categories of works as detailed in Table 2.9.

As the geographical location of both Puducherry/Karaikal Districts and the
neighbouring Districts are similar, executing of similar types of works in the
UT requires attention for creation of durable assets.

2.1.8.4 Wage-material ratio of the works executed under MGNREGA in
the Blocks

Para 12.1.1 of the Guidelines provided that the 60:40 ratio for wage and material
costs should be maintained for all the works taken up by the GPs. Thus, the
expenditure per person-day generation of wage employment should have wage
and material costs in the proportion of 60:40.

As per NREGASoft portal, the wage-material ratio for the works taken up in all
the three Blocks during 2021-24 are given Appendix 2.5.

It could be seen from Appendix 2.5 that the material component utilised for the
works executed during the above period ranged between 0.07 per cent and
14 per cent of the total expenditure with the average Labour Material ratio of
96:4 during 2021-24 in the UT. It implies that none of the Blocks had taken any
major works under the scheme, which require materials, so as to create valuable
assets during the above period.

Further, it could be seen from NREGA Soft portal that only 120 material works'*
were taken up in all the three Blocks during the year 2021-24. The poor wage
material ratio highlights that, most of the works were executed without utilising
materials such as cement, steel, bitumen etc., which resulted in non-creation of
durable assets.

2.1.8.5 Analysis of geo-tagged photographs of selected works

Para 7.16.1 of the Guidelines provides that the geo-tagged time-stamped
photographs of the site before the start of work, at the intermediate stage and
after its completion should be uploaded onto NREGASoft portal for ensuring
transparency, accountability and verifiability in the execution of works.

Audit analysed the geo-tagged photographs of the 87 works of the selected
180 works using Python assisted Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM)'
to detect cases with minimal or no visible change between the three stages of
work and further confirmed by manual analysis.

Mainly gravel road works.

A perceptual metric that evaluates image similarity based on luminance, contrast and
structural features making it a reliable tool for identifying inconsistencies in visual
documentation aligned with human perception.

2.1.8.6 Non-convergence of MGNREGA with other Central/State
Schemes

Para 15 of the Operational Guidelines provides that the objectives of
MGNREGA wiz., creation of durable assets and securing livelihood of rural
households can be facilitated through convergence of MGNREGA works with
resources of other programmes/schemes available with Panchayats and other
line Departments.

Audit noticed that the UT Government had not taken any concrete steps to
achieve inter departmental convergence of MGNREGA works with other line
Departments and the required institutional arrangements were also not
established.

Government replied (August 2025) that steps have been initiated to utilise
MGNREGA for permissible convergence activities.

32


Chapter II — Compliance Audit
2.1.8.7 Non-implementation of the Project UNNATI

The Project ‘UNNATI’ intends to upgrade the skill-base of the MGNREGA
workers and thereby improve their livelihoods, so that they can move from the
current partial employment to full employment and thereby reduce their
dependence on MGNREG Scheme.

It was noticed that in the UT of Puducherry, though 82'7 HHs completed
100 days of work during 2021-24 and were eligible under the Project UNNATI,
none of the HHs were identified by the Department for implementing the
scheme and no trainings were imparted to these eligible beneficiaries which had
resulted in the denial of intended benefits of the scheme to these beneficiaries.

Government replied (August 2025) that eligible beneficiaries is being identified
age-wise and training on skill will be imparted.

2.1.8.8 Non-providing of work site facilities

Para 7.12 of the Guidelines provides that work site facilities (Medical aid,
drinking water and shade) are to be provided at the worksite to the workers
under the Scheme. However, during the beneficiary survey, it was found that
work site facilities were not provided as per the guidelines as stated below:

> 72 (33.33 per cent) beneficiaries stated that no shades were available in
the work sites.

> 62 (28.70 per cent) beneficiaries stated that no drinking water facilities
were provided to them during work hours.

> 87 (40.27 per cent) beneficiaries stated that no first aid medicines were
available in the work sites.

2.1.9 Shortfall in required manpower at GP level

Para 18(2) and 18(4) of guidelines states that the UT has to ensure that at least
one GRS is deployed in every GP and Community Resource Persons may be
attached to the Village Panchayats, to provide additional support. Audit noticed
that as against the requirement of 108 GRS in all GPs, only 93 GRS were
appointed leaving vacancy'* of 15 GRS. Further, Community Resource Persons
were also not attached to the GPs for providing additional support. Moreover,
Mates!’ were also not appointed in any of the three Blocks, which resulted in
extra workload on the GRS, which was also one of the reasons attributable for
non-taking up of greater number of works in many of the GPs.

ss Ariyankuppam - 60 HHs and Villianur - 22 HHs.
is Ariyankuppam: 2, Villianur: 11 and Karaikal: 2.

Person managing the worksite facilities including taking attendance of the workers.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

2.1.10 | Grievance redressal, monitoring and internal control

Monitoring, evaluation and review of the scheme play a critical role in the
success of the MGNREGA. Effective monitoring and evaluation ensure
transparency, accountability and efficient use of resources. It was thus
imperative to have a robust and efficient monitoring, evaluation and review
mechanism of the Scheme.

Audit findings pertaining to the monitoring, evaluation and review of the
scheme are discussed below.

2.1.10.1 Shortfall in the conduct of inspections by the PO/DPO

Para 2.2.1(iv) and 2.3.1(viii) of the guidelines provides that the Programme
Officer (PO) has to monitor and supervise the implementation of works taken
up by the GPs and other implementing agencies within the Block and that the
District Programme Co-ordinator (DPC) has to conduct periodic inspections of
the works in progress and verification of Muster Rolls.

It was, however, noticed that in the Ariyankuppam and Villianur Blocks, the
DPCs as well as the POs of the respective Blocks had not conducted any
inspections in the work sites during 2021-24.

Government replied (August 2025) that inspections are being conducted
regularly by the PO and DPC. The reply is not acceptable as the records
furnished by the Programme Officers stated that a meagre number of inspections
only were conducted by the POs and no inspections were conducted by the DPO
in the Blocks.

2.1.10.2 Non-availability of Quality Control Mechanism

As per para 7.12.1 of the Annual Master Circular 2019-20, a State Quality
Monitoring (SQM) Cell has to be constituted at UT level to monitor and
evaluate at least 10 per cent of the works executed under the scheme. Audit
observed that SQM Cells had not been constituted (November 2024) in the UT
under the scheme.

Government replied (August 2025) that a proposal is under submission for
setting up of a Quality Monitoring Committee exclusively for MGNREGA.

2.1.10.3  Non-constitution of a three tier Vigilance Mechanism

The guidelines provide that at the UT level, at the District level and at the Local
level, Vigilance Cells were to be set-up”. It was, however noticed that no such
vigilance mechanism was in existence (November 2024) in the UT at any level

For receiving complaints for conducting regular field visits and for taking swo-moto
action, etc,

34


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

of implementation. Thus, due to non-setting up of vigilance cell, the possibility
of proactively detecting irregularities in the implementation of the Act could not
be ruled out.

Government replied (August 2025) that process of formal constitution of three
tier vigilance mechanism is under submission to the competent authority.

2.1.10.4 Non-conduct of Social Audit

Para 13.1.4 of the Guidelines provides that UT Government shall conduct Social
Audit once in six months in every GP through people participation with the
support provided by an independent Social Audit Organisation.

However, it was observed that no Social Audit was conducted in the UT since
inception of the MGNREGA which resulted in non-ensuring of transparency,
participation, consultation, accountability and creating awareness amongst the
labourers about their rights and entitlements under the Act.

Government replied (August 2025) that training for the Social Audit team is
currently in progress. Upon completion of the training, the social audit will be
initiated immediately within the current financial year.

2.1.10.5 | Implementation and functioning of the Grievance Redressal
Mechanism

Section 19 of the Act provides that the UT Government shall determine
appropriate grievance redressal mechanisms at the Block and District level for
dealing with any complaint in respect of implementation of the Scheme and lay
down the procedure for disposal of such complaints. Further, Para 13.13.1(x)
of the Guidelines provides that statewide publicity must be made for the
grievance redressal at all levels. However, it was noticed that no separate
grievance redressal mechanism was available and no statewide publicity was
made in the UT of Puducherry.

Further, Section 23(6) of the Act read with Para 13.13.1(iii) of the Guidelines
states that every complaint received shall be entered in the Complaint Register.
It was, however, noticed that no Complaint Registers were maintained at the
GP/Block/District and UT level for monitoring the complaints received and
disposed of.

Government replied (August 2025) that the grievance redressal mechanism
under MGNREGA is currently functioning at all levels. The reply is not
acceptable as no corroborating evidence has been furnished by the Government
for the establishment of separate grievance redressal mechanism.

35


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

2.1.10.6 Functioning of Ombudsperson

As per the Schedule-I (51. No. 30) of the Act read with Para 13.14 of the
Guidelines, the UT Government has to establish the office of Ombudsman in
the available two Districts for expeditious redressal of grievances regarding
implementation of the scheme. However, only one Ombudsperson was
appointed since the inception of the scheme in the Puducherry District and no
complaints had been received by the Ombudsperson.

Further, it was confirmed in the beneficiary survey that the appointment and
functioning of the Ombudsperson was not properly disseminated among the
workers and the public.

Government replied (August 2025) that regarding the constitution of the
Ombudsman at Karaikal District, the matter has been taken up with the
competent authority and necessary action for appointment is under process.

2.101 Conclusion

The implementation of the MGNREG Scheme in the UT was deficient in
several key areas. The UT Government did not frame Rules to carry out the
provisions of MGNREG Act, as required in the Act. The baseline survey, as
prescribed in the guidelines, was not conducted. No separate Annual
Development Plan and Labour Budget were prepared at the GP/Block/District
level. There were significant gaps between the projected and actual PDs
generated, indicating that the Department failed to accurately assess and project
the labour demand to ensure 100 days of employment to all the willing HHs.

Further, the UT Government did not release its proportionate share of
= 3.68 crore towards material component and diverted = 1.24 crore from
material to the administrative component leading to inadequate execution of
material works and non-creation of durable assets. The lack of rules for
payment of unemployment allowance and failure to pay eligible beneficiaries
reflect weak compliance with the statutory provisions. | Employment
opportunities for differently abled persons were insufficient, and no initiatives
were taken to identify or include them.

The employment generated under the scheme was much less than the guaranteed
100 days of wage employment. The average PDs generated were 16.02, 19.61
and 41.69 in the three Blocks during the years 2021-24. Only 82 HHs
were provided 100 days of employment across all the three Blocks. Most of
the works undertaken were limited to desilting and deepening of
water bodies resulting in non-creation of durable assets with material
expenditure ranging between 0.07 per cent and 14 per cent as against the
prescribed norms of 40 per cent.

36


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

There were shortfalls in conduct of inspection of works by District Programme
Officer. Social Audit was not conducted since the inception of the scheme, and
no grievance redressal mechanism, such as complaint register, was maintained
for resolving complaints.

Overall, the deficiencies indicate poor planning, weak financial management,
and ineffective monitoring, undermining the core objectives of the MGNREG
scheme to provide sustainable wage employment and create durable assets.

2.1.12

Recommendations

The Government may

Constitute the institutional mechanisms viz., State Employment
Guarantee Council, District level Technical Committees and Block
Resource Centres as required in the Act and Guidelines.

Establish State Employment Guarantee Fund (SEGF) and frame SEGF
Rules to ensure transparency, efficiency and accountability.

Release its proportional share towards material cost, so that material
works could be executed under the scheme.

Prepare Labour Budget based on the demand for job and ensure that
100 days of guaranteed wage employment is provided to households.

Take necessary steps to pay the wages on time and pay unemployment

allowance to the eligible persons.

Consider executing works which create durable assets like construction
of check dams, roads, Anganwadis etc., with the approval of Gram
Sabha.

Conduct Social Audit as prescribed in the Act and Guidelines to ensure
public accountability in the implementation of the Act and take
immediate steps to create a grievance redressal mechanism to resolve

complaints.

22.1 Introduction

# 2०4 औ। Background of Jal Jeevan Mission

Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) is the flagship scheme launched by Government of
India (Gol) in August 2019. It aimed to provide a Functional Household Tap
Connection (FHTC) to every rural household in the country through
decentralised and integrated water resource management by the Gram
Panchayats, with a key focus on water conservation, source sustainability, and

rainwater storage. The components of JJM are indicated in Chart 2.7.

(Source: JJM Guidelines)

The Union Territory of Puducherry (UTP) consists of four geographically

unconnected regions viz., Puducherry, Karaikal, Mahe and Yanam and is
divided into two Districts of Puducherry and Karaikal. Puducherry District
comprises of Puducherry region and the two outlying administrative units of

Mahe and Yanam.

38


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

The rural areas of UTP are divided into three Blocks viz., Ariyankuppam,
Villianur and Karaikal (two in Puducherry and one in Karaikal) under which
there are 10 Rural Local Bodies viz., Commune Panchayats (CP) i.e., five each
in Puducherry and Karaikal District functioning under the control of Local
Administration Department (LAD).

There are 108 Village/Gram Panchayats (GP) (81 GPs in Puducherry and 27 in
Karaikal) with 246 Villages (Puducherry - 188 and Karaikal - 58). The rural
UTP is depicted in Chart 2.8.

The water supply in rural areas is governed by respective CPs. However, in the
urban pockets of CPs, water supply is under the control of divisions of Public
Works Department (PWD), Puducherry and Karaikal. There were totally
1,00,321 households as of April 2019 in all 10 CPs, out of which 93,506 were
with FHTC before commencement of JJM. During the mission period upto
March 2024, number of households increased to 1,14,969.

22.1.2 Organisational set up

The Secretary to the Government, the administrative head of the Local
Administration Department is the Mission Director, who is assisted by the
Director (LAD), who is responsible for overall implementation and monitoring
of the JIM Scheme in UTP. The Commissioners of CPs are in charge of the
field level implementation of JJM.

The divisions functioning under the PWD, Puducherry and Karaikal were
entrusted with the creation of infrastructure relating to water supply for CPs

39


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

under JJM. On creation of infrastructure, the same were to be handed over to
CPs for further operation and maintenance.

Audit objectives were to assess whether:

implementation, and post operational management of the schemes;

financial resources have been employed in an economic and efficient

assets created through the programme are sustainable for the next two to
three decades and do not compromise water security for future
generations.

Chapter II — Compliance Audit

2.2.1.5 Scope of Audit, Methodology and Sample Selection

The Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) covered the implementation of
JJM in the UTP during the five-year period from 2019-20 to 2023-24. The audit
commenced with an Entry Conference with the Secretary to the Government
(LAD) on 25 July 2024 in which the audit objectives, scope, methodology and
coverage of the Audit were briefed. The audit was conducted during the period
July 2024 to December 2024 involving scrutiny of records at LAD, Public
Health Division, PWD, Puducherry and the Office of the Executive Engineer,
PWD, Karaikal and all the 10 CPs, besides other Offices/Departments
connected with implementation of JJM. An Exit Conference was held on
07 August 2025 with the Secretary to the Government, LAD, Government of
Puducherry, wherein the audit findings of the Subject Specific Compliance
Audit (SSCA) were discussed. The Government replies (October 2025) to the
report have been suitably incorporated in the relevant paragraphs.

All the 10 CPs under three Blocks of both the Districts of UTP were covered in
Audit. Out of the 246 villages, JJM works were carried out in 51 villages.
Accordingly, 50 per cent of 51 villages (26 villages) were taken up for detailed
scrutiny in Audit through Random Sampling Method using IDEA Software
(Ariyankuppam - 6, Villianur - 7 and Karaikal - 13). In the selected villages,
meetings with VWSC and survey of 650 beneficiaries (25 in each village) were
conducted jointly with the Department.

2.2.1.6 Acknowledgement

Audit acknowledges the cooperation and assistance extended by the
UT Government, LAD, Public Health Division, PWD, Puducherry, EE/PWD,
Karaikal and all the CPs in conduct of this Audit.

2.2.2 Output and Outcome Framework of Jal Jeevan Mission

According to Guidelines, the success of JJM is measured based on the primary

output of provision of FHTCs to all rural households by 2024.

A FHTC under this mission has three key characteristics:

> Adequate Quantity: It ensures the supply of at least 55 litres per capita
per day (Iped).

> Prescribed Quality: The water provided meets the BIS:10500 standard

> Regular supply: The water supply is continuous and sustainable in the
long term.

Considering the above, Audit analysed the number of FHTCs provided under
JJM as per the Integrated Management Information System of the Department/
National Mission viz., e-Jalsakthi portal (Portal) and records of CPs.

41


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Further, the functionality of tap connections i.e., regular supply of water with
adequate quantity and required quality was reviewed through conduct of
meetings with the members of VWSC and surveys conducted among
beneficiaries in the sampled villages.

As such, the approach under JJM ensures socio-economic wellbeing of rural
households. The outcome of the Mission were assessed through beneficiary
survey.

The following observations have been made regarding the functioning of the
Mission.

223 Planning

The planning under JJM is based on the long-term plan for the State water
supply sector and addresses the issues pertaining to water security and
sustainability of sources and systems. Accordingly, there should be
transformation from present ‘department-based and construction or
infrastructure development’ to ‘utility based’ i.e., from creation of water supply
infrastructure to water management viz., service delivery.

47


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

The approach, inter alia includes sustainable Operation and Maintenance
(O&M) of the systems, undertaking water budgeting and audits at regular
intervals, cost recovery and reducing the energy charges, etc.

22:31 Non-preparation of State Action Plan

In order to achieve the objectives of JJM, as per Para 3.5(xxiii) of JIM
Operational guidelines, Village Action Plan (VAP) was to be prepared based on
the type of scheme to be taken up in the village to provide FHTC to every rural
household. The VAPs of all villages in a District and all District Action Plans
of all Districts in the State were to be aggregated along with regional water
supply scheme’s/bulk water transfer and treatment plants based on the need to
formulate the State Action Plan (SAP) for the period up to 2024. The strategic
plan under SAP was to detail source-sustainability, functionality, monitoring,
third party inspections for implementation, water measurement, greywater
management and comprehensive O&M plan for the system.

Further, States/UTs were also to prepare an Annual Action Plan (AAP), which
would emerge from the SAP, detailing yearly targets commensurate with the
annual allocation and would be submitted to Department of Drinking Water and
Sanitation for approval.

It was noticed that no SAP was prepared for the Mission period, though AAPs
were prepared for the years 2021-22, 2022-23 and 2023-24 and got approved
by Gol. In response to a specific query, it was stated that as the coverage of
FHTCs under JJM was very limited, the five-year SAP was not prepared for the
Mission period from 2019-2024.

In this context, during the field visit, it was observed that there were villages
facing long-standing water-related issues, as detailed in Paragraphs 2.2.2.2
and 2.2.2.3 (a). Due to the lack of a holistic approach, these problems persisted
even after the implementation of JJM.

The Government also acknowledged (October 2025) that a strategic framework
in the form of SAP could have provided a more integrated approach to
addressing systemic challenges, particularly in villages facing long-standing
water supply issues.

2.2.3.2 Lack of community participation in the implementation of JJM

As per Para 3.5 of Guidelines, a community-led partnership with the UTP is the
key strategy for achieving the mission’s objectives. Also, Para 5.4 of Guidelines
emphasises that the community should play a lead role in planning,
implementation, management, operation and maintenance of in-village water
supply infrastructure, thereby ensuring provision of FHTC to every rural
household.

48


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

However, in the UTP, it was observed that community participation was largely
absent in the implementation of JJM, as detailed below:

(व) Improper constitution of VWSCs

As per Para 4.20.2 of Margdarshika for Gram Panchayat and VWSC to provide
safe drinking water in rural households constituting VWSC/Paani Samitis/User
Groups, will be done by the Gram Sabha in which all sections of the community
to be involved.

However, it was noticed that these committees primarily comprised women
from Self-Help Groups under the National Rural Livelihoods Mission and CP
staff, with no representation of traditional village leaders as recommended by
the Gol. Common villagers were largely excluded from these committees.

(b) Inadequate community involvement in Water Quality Surveillance

As outlined in Chapter 10 of the Guidelines, water quality surveillance should
be conducted by the community using Field Test Kits (FTKs). Although lists
of trained women were uploaded on the portal, discussions with VWSC
members revealed that training was imparted only to four CP tank operators in
the 26 sampled villages, and no women from the concerned villages had
received FTK training. Consequently, community participation in water quality
surveillance was absent.

The above observations indicate a gap between the actual needs of the
community and the schemes implemented, underscoring the importance of
ensuring meaningful community participation in future planning and execution
of works under JJM.

Government replied (October 2025) that FTKs are issued to SHG members who
are also VWSC members and training was imparted to them to use FTKs under
the supervision of CPs.

Reply is not acceptable, during field visit VWSC members stated that FTKs
were not used and further department failed to produce the records of test reports
using FTKs.

2.2.3.3 Absence of water meters impacting effective water management

As per Para 11.1 of Guidelines, it is essential to maintain data on daily water
supply from the source. To achieve this, water meters were to be installed at
water sources and delivery points, which would help reduce water wastage, as
consumption-based billing would encourage efficient usage.

However, it was observed that no water meters were installed in the UTP either
at water source (borewells, sumps, tanks), or households connected with
FHTCs.

49


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

The Government accepted (October 2025) the fact of not having water meters
and decided to have water meter to every household in future.

2.2.3.4 | Non-collection/Short collection of water charges

In the absence of a robust system for assessing water connections in the UTP, it
was admitted that 20,025 tap connections (20 per cent) out of 99,993 tap
connections in 2020 were unauthorised and not levied water charges.

Thus, due to failure to implement effective measures for streamlining the
collection process resulted in accumulation of arrears of water charges
amounting to % 21.12 crore® as of March 2024. The Government accepted
(August 2025) short collection of water charges during exit conference.

2.2.3.5 Non-conduct of Social Audit under JIM

As per Para 5.4(xviii) of the Guidelines, GPs and VWSCs are mandated to
conduct social audits to ensure transparency, accountability and that the benefits
of JJM reach the intended beneficiaries.

During the mission period, 87 works™ have been executed under JJM, incurring
an expenditure of = 10.01 crore. However, it was observed that no social audits
were conducted for these projects. The absence of social audits undermines
transparency and makes it difficult to assess whether the objectives of JJM were
fully achieved and the benefits effectively reached the targeted rural
households.

Government replied (October 2025) that the lack of community participation
was due to absence of elected representatives in the UT of Puducherry. Further
stated that since no remuneration is provided to VWSC member, it was difficult
to ensure their full and active participation.

2.2.4 Post operational management and sustainability

Post Operational Management and sustainability ensures that created water
supply systems remain functional and efficient to the needs through formulation
of Operation and Maintenance Policy, source sustainability measures and
Greywater Management. The deficiencies noticed during the conduct of Audit
is pointed out below.

2.2.4.1 Gaps in community contribution towards Project Costs

As per paragraphs 6.1.2 and 7.11 of Guidelines, the community is required to
contribute 10 per cent of the capital cost through cash, kind or labour for the
implementation of piped water supply infrastructure and related source

7 Puducherry: = 17.08 crore and Karaikal: 3.04 crore.
& Completed: 81 and In progress: 6.

50


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

development. This community contribution, along with the willingness of at
least 80 per cent of households in the village, is a pre-requisite for initiating any
water supply scheme.

As per information furnished by LAD, the final value of works completed and
under process was % 6.24 crore for which an amount of 2 0.62 crore was to be
collected towards community contribution. However, only an amount of
20.18 crore was collected for works executed under JJM, leaving % 0.44 crore’
uncollected from eight CPs.

The beneficiary survey conducted by Audit revealed that 82 per cent of the
beneficiaries surveyed were aware of the payment of community contribution
towards the capital cost of the work executed under JJM.

Government replied (October 2025) that due to the absence of elected council
members, community contribution could not be fully realised as only a few
Commune Panchayats made marginal collections. Government further replied
that it has decided to provide a grant towards the community contribution in
addition to the State share.

2.2.4.2 Absence of source sustainability measures

Para 6.1 of the JJM Guidelines emphasises the importance of long-term,
sustainable planning for drinking water sources to ensure safe water availability,
even during distress periods through strategies like dedicated borewell recharge
structures, rainwater harvesting and rejuvenation of existing water bodies
ideally in convergence with schemes like MGNREGS, MPLAD, MLALAD and
CSR initiatives.

In the Districts of Puducherry and Karaikal, groundwater is the sole source of
drinking water. Further, the Central Ground Water Board’s March 2022 report
classified the groundwater status as critical and noted no significant change in
overall groundwater resources of UT of Puducherry since 2017.

However, no rainwater harvesting systems were implemented in any water
sources across the Districts, including in areas facing severe water shortages
during summer. Also, the report of water test done by Audit, across the selected
villages revealed that 81 per cent of the tested samples (as detailed in
Appendix 2.8) had TDS levels above the desirable threshold, which could be
minimised through recharge of sources.

Government replied (October 2025) that different activities carried out for
desilting of tanks, ponds and rivers.

# Bahour - रैं 6.60 lakh, Mannadipet - हैं 0.77 lakh, Nettapakkam - हैं 0.52 lakh,
Kottucherry - = 5.8 lakh, Nedungadu - हैं 2.13 lakh, Neravy - हैं 7.14 lakh,
T.R. Pattinam - = 4.68 lakh, Thirunallar - = 16.17 lakh.

51


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Reply is not acceptable since the JJM guidelines emphasises the importance of
borewell recharge structures, rainwater harvesting and rejuvenation of water
bodies, but no such type of activities was carried out with convergence of
various schemes under JJM.

2:2:4.3 Lack of Greywater management

As per Para 3.3 of the JJM Guidelines, greywater’ management is a key
objective. Accordingly, greywater management has to be done funding support
from schemes like Swachh Bharat Mission - Grameen (SBM-G).

During the field visit, it was observed that FHTCs were installed outside houses,
near doorsteps (as shown in Pictures 2.8 and 2.9) in all the test-checked
beneficiaries houses, instead of at the recommended three delivery points viz.,
kitchen, washing/bathing area, and toilet under JJM, which led to untreated
greywater flowing into streets, causing water stagnation, unsanitary conditions,
mosquito breeding, and water wastage.

the Rural Development Department on installation of community soak pits in
four village.

Government replied (October 2025) that DRDA have their own action plan for
establishing community soak pits.

Reply is not acceptable as scheme guidelines envisages that funds can be
sourced by convergence with other schemes. However, no such attempt was
made from the nodal agency.

22.5.4 Non-obtaining of allocated Gol share

As per Chapter 7 of the Guidelines, JJM is a time-bound mission, requiring
robust financial planning and prudent utilisation of funds for successful
implementation. The fund-sharing pattern between Gol and GoP is 90:10 under
JJM. Annual allocation was to be released in two equal instalments, with each
instalment being disbursed in two tranches. The first instalment (50 per cent of

The support funds are available for States to carry out the functions such as
Information, Education and Communication (IEC) among local communities, Human
Resource Development (HRD), training and skill development; mobilisation of local
communities; third party inspection.

WQMAS activities include conduct of awareness and education programmes on water
quality in Schools, Anganwadi centres, Health centres, GPs/PRIs, etc., enabling
communities to undertake presumptive testing of water quality using FTKs.

Though the Audit period covers from 2019-20 in the UT, the first AAP was prepared
only in 2020-21.
