Although the JJM was implemented in August 2019, the nodal Department for
its implementation viz., LAD was designated by the UTP only in March 2020.
Further, the National Rural Drinking Water Programme (NRDWP), the
previous water supply scheme which was scheduled to be completed in the UTP
by March 2020, was finalised only in August 2020, due to delay in submission
of Utilisation Certificates (UCs). Furthermore, the UT-level institutional
mechanisms for approving plans and strategies under JJM were established only
in January 2021.

As a result, the AAPs for 2019-20 and 2020-21 were not submitted.

in July 2021, causing a delay of two years from the launch of JJM and resulting

eventually cancelled due to administrative reasons.

expired by then. An extension of the tender validity was requested by PWD

remained unresolved, affecting 899 HHs. Since the opportunity to complete the
project under JIM was missed, the UT would have to bear an additional
financial burden of at least = 3.78 crore (90 per cent of the present tender cost)
from its own funds.

2.2.6.2 Rural Water Supply Works taken up by availing loans from
NABARD instead of inclusion under Jal Jeevan Mission

As per para 3.6.1 of the JJM operational guidelines, Village Action Plans should
be prepared by Gram Panchayat based on baseline survey, resource mapping

z Mannadipet, Sompet and Mummarampet.

58


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

and felt needs of the village community. The VAPs include need assessment of
the water required in the village and the available resources.

Ona scrutiny of files relating to water supply works taken up by the PH Division
of the PWD, it was observed that six augmentation works were taken up due to
insufficient water supply in various villages by availing loans from NABARD
during 2019-2024. Although, the proposals for taking up of these six works had
been submitted by the Commissioners of CPs to the PWD well before the
inception of the JJM scheme, however, the works were not included under the
ambit of JJM scheme.

Audit observed that taking up of rural water supply works through NABARD
loans instead of under JJM, a Centrally Sponsored Scheme, resulted in a
financial burden of % 14.69 crore on the UT Government as detailed in
Appendix 2.10.

Government replied (October 2025) that since the audit findings were connected
with PWD and reply will be submitted in consultation with PWD.

The reply is not acceptable since the proposals were made well before the
implementation of JJM in UTP and Department while planning could have
brought all these works under the purview of JJM.

22:7 Water quality monitoring and surveillance

Under JJM Guidelines vide Para No. 4.5, water quality monitoring involves
testing of water samples collected from water sources by Department in water
quality testing laboratories while surveillance is undertaken by community
using FTKs, sanitary inspection, etc.

In the UTP, two Public Health Laboratories are available for testing chemical
contamination one in Puducherry and Karaikal, both under the administrative
control of the PWD.

2271 Water quality testing

Chapter 10 of the Guidelines emphasises the importance of water quality testing
for monitoring the operation of water supply systems, ensuring the safety of
drinking water, investigating disease outbreaks, validating processes and
implementing preventive measures. Under the Water Quality Management
System (WQMS) framework of the JJM, 100 per cent testing of all drinking
water sources is mandated, along with comprehensive reporting for public
dissemination.

Further, as per Para 2.1.2 of the Handbook for Monitoring Water Quality of
Piped Drinking Water Supply to Rural Households issued by the Government
of India, samples from groundwater-based schemes should be collected at a
convenient location after the outlet of the storage structure feeding the

59


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

distribution system. Water sources are required to be tested at least twice
annually for both chemical and bacteriological contamination.

Table 2.21.

Chapter II — Compliance Audit

600 mg/l (in the absence of alternative source) and in respect of 27 per cent of

though within the permissible limit of 1,000 mg/l (in the absence of alternative

Similarly, the bacteriological testing of water was found to be insufficient. The
number of water samples collected randomly by Public Health Centres from
public and household taps for bacteriological testing, conducted by the Health
Department's laboratory, declined from 3,038 to 2,889 in Puducherry and in
Karaikal it increased from 1,503 to 1,599 in during the audit period.

The findings highlight the urgent need for effective water management
strategies. In the absence of adequate rainwater harvesting and conservation
measures, water sources may face depletion and key water quality parameters
such as TDS, total Hardness and Chloride may exceed permissible limits in the
future.

22962 Non-dissemination of test results

Para 5.4 of the Guidelines stipulates that the results of water quality testing must
be disseminated to the community by the VWSC. While laboratory testing was
carried out by the PWD, the results were not shared with the community and no
awareness initiatives were undertaken regarding the quality of the water
supplied to the villages.

Government replied (October 2025) that action would be initiated to
disseminate the test results.

2.2.7.3. Lack of water quality surveillance by community using FTK

Chapter 10 of the Guidelines and Para 6.3.6 of the Water Quality Monitoring
and Surveillance Framework specify that water quality surveillance activities
should include the use of FTKs at the Gram Panchayat (GP) level to assess
contamination levels. Positively tested samples must then be referred to the
nearest water quality testing laboratory for confirmation. FTKs play a crucial
role in the initial screening of physicochemical contamination in water and also
help in raising community awareness about drinking water quality.

Under the AAP for 2022-23, an allocation of = 0.41 crore was approved for the
WQM&S component. Accordingly, in August 2022, a decision was taken to
procure 123 FTKs for 50 per cent of villages in Puducherry and Karaikal. The
Tamil Nadu Water Supply and Drainage (TWAD) Board was approached in
November 2022 for supply of FTKs. However, TWAD_ informed
(December 2022) that the FTKs were out of stock and would require six months
to supply after order confirmation. As a result, order for 123 FTKs under the
first phase was finally placed through Government e-Market in October 2023.

61


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

The FTKs were procured in December 2023 at a total cost of = 1.85 lakh and
subsequently distributed to the CPs in March 2024, after a delay of nearly one
year and three months from the initial decision to procure them. The delay is
significant considering that the shelf life of the FTKs is only 12 months
(upto November 2024).

The CPs distributed the FTKs to tank operators, instead of utilising them for
community-led surveillance through women from Village Panchayat, who were
to be trained in using FTKs. It was noticed that training on FTK usage was
conducted only in 11 out of the 26 selected villages and no FTK-based water
testing was carried out by VWSC members in any of the CPs.

The Government accepted (October 2025) that FTKs were issued belatedly to
the CPs.

2.2.7.4 Non-prioritisation of quality affected villages

As per Para 2.5 of Guidelines, objectives of JJM emphasise that provision of
FHTCs in quality affected areas, i.e., wherein the supplied water containing
iron, arsenic and fluoride beyond the admissible limit, are to be prioritised and
potable water is to be provided through FHTC by March 2021.

A review of water sample testing data from the PWD laboratory, Puducherry
for the period 2020-24 revealed that 42 water samples had an iron content
exceeding the permissible limit (>1 mg per litre) across the five CPs in
Puducherry as detailed in Table 2.22.

62


Chapter II — Compliance Audit
BIS Non-cleaning of OHTs regularly

Operation and maintenance are crucial for ensuring the functionality of
household tap connections. Para 6.2 of the Guidelines mandates that States
must diligently adhere to Central Public Health and Environmental Engineering
Organisation (CPHEEO) norms for the operation and maintenance of water
supply schemes. The best way for maintaining water quality is regular cleaning
of Overhead Tanks (OHTs) and sumps.

In the 10 CPs, there are 204 OHTs and 120 sumps. However, it was observed
in respect of nine CPs* whose details were made available that:

> 11 numbers of the OHTs and sumps in nine CPs had not been cleaned
even once during the past five years (2019-20 to 2023-24).

> 135 out of 204 OHTs and sumps were cleaned only one to four times
during the same period.

These lapses in routine maintenance pose potential risks to water quality and
public health, highlighting the need for strict adherence to CPHEEO norms and
regular monitoring of cleaning schedules.

Government replied (October 2025) that the regular cleaning of OHTs is being
done in all CPs and will instruct to maintain proper records for document
verification.

Reply is not acceptable, as the response of the Commune Panchayats establish
that the OHTs were not cleaned at regular intervals and 11 number of OHTs
were not cleaned during audit period.

2.2.7.6 Ineffective monitoring by State Water and Sanitary Mission

The State Water and Sanitary Mission (SWSM) was constituted as a Society as
per the Guidelines and was inter-alia responsible for (i) providing policy
guidance and overall implementation, (ii) finalisation of SAP, financial
planning including ensuring timely utilisation of fund and no parking of fund
and (iii) formation of Village Water and Sanitary Mission, etc.

However, it was noticed that although the Apex Committee (AC) under SWSM
was required to meet at least twice a year, it met only three times during the
mission period from 2019 to 2024, primarily for submission of AAPs under
JJM. Further, it was observed that the ineffective functioning of SWSM had
resulted in several deficiencies in implementation of JJM viz., non-preparation
of SAP, non-obtaining Gol share due to delayed submission of proposal, UC
etc., (as discussed in Paragraph 2.2.5.1) and belated proposal of augmentation
works in the AAP for 2022-23 at the fag end of the Mission.

8 Villianur Commune Panchayat did not furnish details relating to non-cleaning of

OHTs.

63


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

2.2.7.7 Non-formation of grievance redressal mechanism

Para 3.6.2 of Guidelines requires creation of an independent regulatory body
which would deal with the redressal of grievances of aggrieved user.

However, no grievance redressal body was formed under JJM in UTP. Toa
specific query, it was replied (March 2025) that the same is under progress in
co-ordination with PWD.

2.2.8 Good practice

During the survey, the beneficiaries expressed their appreciation for the support
and coordination provided by the Implementation Support Agencies, viz., CPs,
in effectively addressing regular water supply issues like proactive engagement,
follow-up on complaints and assistance, thereby ensuring smoother and
sustained service delivery.

2.2.9 Conclusion

The implementation of the Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) in the UT of Puducherry
exhibited significant deficiencies in planning, financial management, water
quality assurance, and achievement of intended outcomes. Audit revealed that,
contrary to the claim of 100 per cent FHTC coverage reported on e-Jalsakthi
portal, 3,459 out of 24,927 households in 26 sampled villages did not actually
have FHTCs as of March 2024.

Deficiencies were also observed in convergence with other schemes, water
quality monitoring, dissemination of test results, community participation and
grievance redressal mechanism. Due to delays in submission of proposals and
non-utilisation of available funds, the UT received only ₹ 9.53 crore from Gol
during 2019-24, against allocation of = 55.19 crore.

Out of 92 works approved under JJM, five works were not taken up, 81 works
were completed and six works remained incomplete. Further, in respect of three
works, audit noticed that the works were not executed as planned, with
deficiencies in provisions of FHTCs.

The absence of water meters at both source and household levels further
restricted the ability to monitor supply and to ensure targeted delivery and
monitoring of outputs.

Overall, the shortcomings in execution, fund management, and monitoring
mechanism indicate weak implementation and oversight, undermining the goal
of providing safe, adequate, and sustainable tap water supply to every household
under the JJM.



Chapter II — Compliance Audit

2.2.10 Recommendations

The Government may

. expedite the pending augmentation works to enable public derive
benefits out of them and ensure quantity and quality of water supply.

. identify left out households and provide them with FHTCs

. prioritise water quality testing at all levels to ensure that water of
mandated quality is supplied to public

. implement rainwater harvesting systems in water sources to safeguard
water quality and sustained use of water.

. involve village community in preparation of proposal for the scheme and
in testing of water using FTKs.

° install FHTCs inside houses as envisaged in the scheme.

. install water meters at sources and household to ensure delivery of
55 LPCD of water as envisaged under JJM.

2.3 Unfruitful expenditure

EPARTMENT

2.3.1 Non-fulfilment of objective due to non-utilisation of land
for college building

Land acquired at a cost of = 13.46 crore for Indira Gandhi College of Arts and
Science, Puducherry remained unutilised for over 15 years due to absence of
approach road, leaving the objective unfulfilled.

Indira Gandhi College of Arts and Science (IGCAS), Puducherry, established
in 2001, has been functioning in the premises of Government Girls Higher
Secondary School (GGHSS), Kathirkamam, since its inception. To house the
college in its own building, the Education Secretariat accorded
(November 2003) administrative sanction for acquisition of land at
Kathirkamam in Oulgaret Revenue Village. The Revenue Department (RD)
initiated (September 2006) land acquisition proposals and the Site Selection
Committee, headed by the Secretary, Education Department, in its meeting held
during May 2008 unanimously decided to acquire land™ in Oulgaret village.
The RD certified (July 2009) that the land was free from all encumbrances and
subsequently, the Directorate of Higher and Technical Education (DHTE) took

2 hectares 99 acres and 25 cents.

65


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

possession (July 2009) of the land from RD after paying an amount of
= 3.90 crore. Further, DHTE paid (July 2021 and December 2022) enhanced
compensation of = 9.56 crore (including interest) pursuant to the orders of the
Hon’ble Civil Court. Despite an expenditure of = 13.46 crore, the land acquired
for IGCAS has remained unutilised for its intended purpose, even after lapse of
15 years.

Scrutiny of records (May 2024) in the Higher and Technical Education
Department revealed the following:

e The Site Selection Committee (May 2008) had recommended
acquisition of the land despite being aware that the existing approach
road was narrow and inadequate. Subsequent requests (June 2011 to
August 2015) from the Principal, IGCAS, for transfer/acquisition of
Government poramboke or private land to provide direct access from
Vazhudhavur Road were not acted upon. The Puducherry Planning
Authority (February 2024) also observed that the access road width
(6.10 m) was below the prescribed minimum of 9 m. However, the RD
proceeded with acquisition of the land without ensuring availability of
an adequate access road. Further, joint inspection (May 2023) revealed
that the site was low-lying, waterlogged and prone to inundation,
rendering it unsuitable for construction of institutional buildings.

e Despite possession of the acquired land since 2009, no construction was
initiated. In a meeting held on 29 December 2015 by the Chief Minister
with the Commissioner-cum-Secretary (Education) and the Member
Secretary, Pondicherry Society for Higher Education, it was decided to
continue in the existing campus by constructing an academic Block,
leaving the acquired land unutilised. An academic Block was
constructed (June 2019) within the (GGHSS) campus at a cost of
= 2.38 crore. Subsequently, owing to the shortage of classrooms in
GGHS campus and increase intake of students, the college was shifted
in July 2022 to Indira Gandhi Government Higher Secondary School,
Indira Nagar where it continues to function in the premises not owned
by it.

e Thus, the inappropriate decision of the Site Selection Committee to
acquire land for IGCAS resulted in expenditure of = 13.46 crore on land
acquisition and compensation. Due to poor planning at the selection
stage, the land acquired for IGCAS has remained unutilised for over
15 years owing to lack of a proper approach road, low-lying site
conditions. Additional expenditure of = 2.38 crore on construction of
academic Block, coupled with the continued absence of a permanent
campus, deprived the students of basic amenities. In absence of its own
building the college could not obtain additional assistance under Section
12(B) of the UGC Act, 1956 and the students were deprived of adequate

66


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

classrooms and laboratories facilities. Thus, failure to establish the
college building in its own campus even after 15 years frustrated the
long standing objective of shifting the college to the acquired land.

Government replied (September 2025) that the Department was constrained to
construct the building in its own land until December 2022 due to Court
proceedings relating to Enhanced Compensation. Further, the Government
replied that since the college was functioning in GGHSS for long years, the
additional building was constructed due to dearth of classrooms for school
students.

Reply is not tenable as the Government did not address key issues such as
absence of approach road, non-construction of college building on its own land
and hardship faced by the college students due to the cramped environment.
Further, the Hon’ble Justice of Madras High Court, expressed (July 2019)
serious concern over the land remaining unutilised for the past 10 years. Even
when the principal of the college approached (February 2024) Puducherry
Planning Authority (PPA) for construction of the college building, it expressed
concerns regarding inadequate approach road, confirming that the issue
remained unresolved despite an expenditure of = 13.46 crore.

Recommendation:

The Government should take measures and ensure that land acquired is utilised
for the stated objective and not to be left unutilised.

ED TRIBES

2.3.2 Unfruitful expenditure on Multipurpose Hall

Due to non-provision of necessary funds by the Adi-Dravidar Welfare and
Scheduled Tribes Welfare Department, incomplete structure of a Multipurpose
Hall constructed up to grade beam level at a cost of = 4.46 crore has remained
incomplete for the past four and a half years, rendering the expenditure
unfruitful.

CPWD Works Manual, 2014 (Paragraph 2.1) lists administrative approval,
expenditure sanction, technical sanction and availability of funds as
pre-requisites for execution of works and provides that no work should normally
be commenced without these pre-requisites. Paragraph 4.1.4(11)(a) of the
Manual provides that execution of the works will depend upon the receipt of the
funds through authorisation or through allocation well in time by the client
Department.

67


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

The Adi-Dravidar Welfare and Scheduled Tribes Welfare (ADW&STW)
Department accorded (March 2019) administrative sanction for construction of
Multipurpose Hall at Kirumampakkam, Pillayarkuppam Revenue Village,
Puducherry for = 13.86 crore, to be executed by Public Works Department
(PWD). Based on the Detailed Project Report (DPR) prepared by Housing and
Urban Development Corporation (HUDCO) and technical sanction of PWD, the
ADW&STW Department accorded (August 2019) expenditure sanction for
% 13.86 crore for the work and released @ five crore as first instalment of the
estimated cost. After due tender process, the work was awarded (August 2019)
to the lowest bidder for = 9.45 crore. The site for the work was handed over to
the contractor (November 2019), who commenced the work immediately.

The payment for the first part bill submitted (March 2020) for the work executed
by the contractor for an amount of = 1.74 crore was not released even by
September 2020. Consequently, the contractor expressed concerns to the PWD,
requested interest for delayed payment and demanded current market value for
all the items to be executed. Subsequently, the work was completed upto grade
beam level (Pictures 2.13 and 2.14) valuing % 3.74 crore (December 2020),
after which the contractor stopped the work due to non-payment of dues.

Pictures 2.13 and 2.14: Incomplete structure of Multipurpose Hall

In this regard, audit observed the following:

During 2019-20, the ADW&STW Department requested (November 2019) the
Finance Department to provide = 5.05 crore, however, only = 2.63 crore was
re-appropriated (March 2020) to the Head of Account. Similarly, during
2020-21, a provision of = two crore was made under Negotiated Loan**
component for the work. The proposal (December 2020) of the ADW&STW
Department to avail a Negotiated Loan of 12.47 crore from HUDCO, with the
balance of = 1.39 crore from UT funds was rejected by the Finance Department
citing high rate of interest. Instead, = 2.20 crore was allotted (February 2021)
in RE 2020-21, which was fully utilised for payment (February 2021) to the
contractor. During 2021-22, though the ADW&STW Department sought
(April 2021) ₹ 11.24 crore, only = 1.60 crore was provided through
re-appropriation. Of this, हैं 1.54 crore was paid (February 2022) to the
contractor, after the work had been foreclosed in September 2021.

8 Loan obtained from Central Financial Institutions like HUDCO, NABARD, Power
Finance Corporation and Rural Electrification Corporation for specified purposes.

68


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

During 2022-23, interest for delayed payment of bills, amounting to
% 0.23 crore was paid (March 2023) to the contractor, as recommended by the
Dispute Redressal Committee (DRC).

Hence, it was observed that non-provision of adequate funds by ADW&STW
Department, as required under the CPWD Works Manual, led to stoppage of
work and unfruitful expenditure of = 4.46 crore**. Further, the incomplete
structure has remained exposed to nature for a period of more than four and a
half years, rendering it prone to deterioration.

Government replied (September 2025) that the fund requested by the
ADW&STW Department had not been provided by the Finance Department due
to financial constraints and priority and need based expenditure, which arose
due to COVID pandemic.

The reply is not acceptable, as review of the budget allotment, expenditure and
savings under the Grant “Building Programmes” revealed savings of
₹ 35.88 crore, = 20.80 crore and = 34.51 crore during the period 2019-20,
2020-21 and 2021-22 respectively. These savings were not re-appropriated for
construction of Multipurpose Hall despite requests from the ADW&STW
Department. No further action was taken by the Department to obtain funds for
completing the balance works in the subsequent years. Thus, the objective of
constructing the Multipurpose Hall for the benefit of Adi-Dravidar and
Scheduled Tribe people remained unachieved.

Recommendation:

. The Department should ensure the availability of funds before
commencement of work to avoid interruptions and ensure their
completion on time. Further, earnest efforts should be taken to utilise

the existing structure and convert it into a usable asset.

DEPARTMENT

2.3.3 Unfruitful expenditure due to idling of hostel building for
more than five years since construction

Hostel constructed for Other Backward Classes girls remained unutilised, since
inauguration, due to its isolated location resulting in unfruitful expenditure of
= 1.98 crore and additional expenditure of = 0.93 crore towards construction of
compound wall and cost escalation.

Government of India (Gol), Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment
sanctioned (September 2004 and December 2008) ₹ 74.20 lakh for construction
of hostel for Other Backward Class (OBC) Boys and Girls at Pillaiyarkuppam
Revenue Village, Bahour, Puducherry, based on the proposal of the Social
Welfare Department (SWD) of the Government of Puducherry (GoP). The Gol
also instructed the Union Territory Government to ensure completion of the
hostel by December 2009. Later, the GoP accorded (May 2012) administrative
sanction of हैं 4.26 crore (estimated by Public Works Department for
100 students) for execution of “Government BC Girls Hostel” and conveyed
expenditure sanction of = 74.20 lakh only for the work to be executed by the
Executive Engineer (EE), Buildings and Roads (North) Division, PWD.

PWD, however, insisted (July 2012) that the SWD obtain expenditure sanction
for the balance amount of 2 3.52 crore, as the work could not be taken up without
expenditure sanction for the entire project cost of = 4.26 crore. The Director of
SWD, however, stated (July 2012) that expenditure sanction for the balance
amount could not be obtained due to shortfall in allocation of funds in the budget
under the head “Building Programme” and hence requested PWD to commence
the construction of hostel building. The PWD was of the firm view
(August 2013) that it would not be possible to commence the work without
obtaining expenditure sanction for the entire estimated amount.

In view of the shortage of funds, the Chief Secretary, GoP suggested in the Plan
Expenditure Review Meeting (August 2013) to scale down the estimate to
accommodate 50 OBC students. Accordingly, the Director, SWD approached
(October 2013) PWD to furnish a revised estimate for = 2.50 crore
50 student’s hostel. The land for construction of the building was handed over
to PWD by SWD in November 2013.

Meanwhile, based on the discussion between the Secretary, SWD and the
EE/PWD the estimate was further pruned (December 2014) to = 1.80 crore for
construction of a hostel building with a plinth area of 600 sq.m. to accommodate
only 18 inmates. Accordingly, the GoP accorded (August 2015) modified

70


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

expenditure sanction for = 1.80 crore (CSS funds: = 0.74 crore and UT funds:
= 1.06 crore).

Tenders were invited (September 2015), and work order was issued
(December 2015) to the successful bidder for a value of = 1.25 crore.

During the earth excavation (February 2016) by the contractor, the ground water
table level was encountered at a depth of one meter from existing ground level,
causing sliding of the soil from the sides of the foundation pit. Accordingly,
PWD, revised (November 2016) the design, which resulted in execution of
additional quantities such as foundation concrete, steel reinforcement for
foundation, earthwork, centering, change in tile design etc., involving an
additional expenditure of = 70 lakh.

The work resumed in January 2017 and was completed only in October 2019 at
a cost of = 1.98 crore, including electrification, contingencies etc. The hostel
building was handed over (December 2019) to the Director of Backward Classes
and Minorities Welfare and was inaugurated in October 2020. However, no
students have been admitted to the hostel since its inauguration.

The following observations are made in this regard.

(i) In the proposal sent (May 2008) to the Ministry of Social Justice and
Empowerment, Gol, the Director of Social Welfare stated that the Backward
Class Students Hostel at Pillaiyarkuppam Village had been proposed, as desired
by the Hon’ble Minister for Social Welfare. Hence, it is evident that no demand
survey was conducted by the Department to assess the anticipated number of
school/college students who would benefit from the hostel.

(ii) The Director for the Welfare of the Backward Classes and Minorities
requested (December 2021) the PWD to carry out additional works in the hostel
viz., compound wall with barbed wire, security staff room, laying of metal road,
closing the courtyard to avoid rain water stagnation, drainage facility, water
harvesting and necessary lighting arrangements. Though the provision of
compound wall to the building was very essential in view of the fact that the
hostel was constructed to house girls, it was constructed only in August 2023 at
a cost of % 0.63 crore, after almost four years from handing over of the building.

(iii) | The EE/PWD, Buildings and Roads (North) Division had informed the
Director of Social Welfare as early as in September 2013 that the site acquired
by the SWD was isolated and had no road connectivity. It was further noted
that issuance of newspaper advertisement (June 2023) and contacting schools
and colleges (July 2023) did not yield any results as the surrounding private
schools and colleges had their own furnished hostels and the parents were also
not willing to send their children to the isolated location of the hostel. This
indicates that the hostel was constructed at an inappropriate site without any
need assessment.

71


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

(iv) Further, the Dispute Redressal Committee convened by the
Chief Engineer/PWD, based on the contractor’s claim awarded (June 2023)
= 30.23 lakh towards cost escalation (cost of cement and steel) payable to the
contractor, under Clause 10CA of the Agreement, which was paid by the
Department in March 2025.

Thus, the Hostel constructed for the Backward Class students has remained
vacant for more than five years due to the absence of a need assessment and
improper site selection, resulting in an unfruitful expenditure of = 2.91 crore.

Government replied (September 2025) that the delay occurred in construction
of hostel was due to fact that no additional funds were available for construction
of hostel with the limited funds from Gol. Further, Government stated that
efforts are being taken to accommodate students in the hostel.

Recommendation:

° The Government may ensure that a demand assessment is carried out
and proper justification for utilisation is established prior to
construction of hostels, to avoid unfruitful expenditure.

2.4 Infructuous expenditure

2.4.1 Imprudent planning in construction of Western byepass led
to infructuous expenditure

Imprudent planning of PWD in construction of Western byepass
(Phase-I - Reach-I1) in Karaikal resulted in infructuous expenditure of
= 2.64 crore.

As per 47.4.8 (2) of CPWD Works Manual, 2014, no outlay for civil works
should be proposed before land is actually made available by the Administrative
Ministry concerned. Due to increased traffic density, the movement of heavy
vehicles, congestion during festive seasons and the development of major
industries in the Karaikal region, a proposal was made to construct a Western
byepass road. The proposed Western bypass crosses the Railway land of
Karaikal Peralam Railway track at LC 20A@ 20.635 km.

Chapter II — Compliance Audit

(February 2015) and expenditure sanction (March 2015) of = 21.20 crore for the

financial assistance through a loan
from HUDCO. Technical

C WORKS AND TOURISM DE

25.1 Non-ayailing of Gol funds for construction of pathway

Due to failure on the part of PWD which led to foreclosure of the work of
Development of Pathways and Ghat along Godavari River in Yanam, the UT
of Puducherry could not avail Gol funds to the tune of % 2.31 crore.

The Ministry of Tourism (MoT), Government of India (Gol) launched the
Swadesh Darshan Scheme (SDS) in 2014-15, under which financial assistance
(fully funded by Gol) was provided to State/UT Governments/Central Agencies
for development of tourism infrastructure at various destinations. One of the
themes under the scheme was “Development of Spiritual Circuit”.

Based on the Detailed Project Report (DPR) submitted by the Director of
Tourism (DoT), Government of Puducherry (GoP), the Gol sanctioned
(June 2017) ₹ 40.68 crore for Development of Spiritual Circuit in Puducherry.
One of the projects sanctioned under the Spiritual Circuit was the “Development
of Pathways and Ghat along Godavari River in Yanam”, for a length of
1,400 meters on the left side of the Shivam Holy Bath at a project cost of
हें 6.20 crore. The project included the works of (i) Formation of pathways and
(ii) Providing (a) Center Median (b) Greenery (c) Granite benches (d) Steps and
(e) Illumination.

The Department of Industrial Development (Tourism), GoP accorded
(September 2017) expenditure sanction of = 6.20 crore and further sanctioned
an initial expenditure of = 1.24 crore (20 per cent Central Financial Assistance
released by Gol). The project was executed through the Public Works
Department (PWD), which called for tenders (December 2017) and awarded
(March 2018) the work to the lowest bidder for = 5.07 crore. As per the
agreement, the work was scheduled to commence in April 2018 and completed
by November 2018. However, the project was delayed due to seasonal floods
in Gowthami Godavari river, execution of work ‘Fabrication of MS foot bridge
across the Coringa River’, non-availability of gravel and COVID pandemic.

After completing about 55 per cent of the work, the contractor stopped
(March 2021) the work, citing obstruction from the farmers of the adjoining
areas for the formation of the pathway along the shore from chainage 110 meters
to 400 meters. However, no written representation from the farmers or
documentary evidence supporting this claim was available. By that time, only
1,000 meters of the pathway (with side slope revetment and CC road pathway
with kerb wall) had been completed at a cost of = 2.73 crore, against the total
length of 1,400 meters. The contractor stopped the work (March 2021) and the

75


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024
balance works included construction of the remaining length of pathways from

chainage 110 to 400 meters and the provision of greenery, granite benches, and
illumination”, remained incomplete. (Pictures 2.17 and 2.18).

Map Camera

Picture 2.17: Unfinished work (pathway laid Picture 2.18: Unfinished work (Gravel Road)
without provision of facilities)

The contractor requested (April 2021) the Department to foreclose the work,
citing the objections raised by the neighbouring landowners/farmers. The
contractor further stated that the remaining tourist infrastructure works such as
landscaping, illumination and provision of benches etc., could not be taken up,
as major flood protection works under the Central Sector Scheme (CSS) were
to be resumed along the riverside part of the above pathway. However, Audit
observed that only in May 2025, a multidisciplinary team from Gol visited
Yanam for study of flood control/protection works to be undertaken under the
CSS, and that no such major works were carried out during 2021-22 to
2024-25, as claimed by the contractor. Therefore, based on the request of the
contractor the project was foreclosed by the PWD in August 2022.

In the meantime, the Gol reduced (June 2021) the project cost of the Spiritual
Circuit to हैं 34.96 crore from the originally sanctioned = 40.68 crore due to
non-taking up of certain works. However, it was noticed that the project cost
for the ‘Development of Pathways and Ghat along Godavari River in Yanam’
was not reduced. Gol instructed (January 2022) GoP to complete the remaining
works and settle bills March 2022 and informed no further funds will be released
by Gol.

During the review meetings, the Ministry of Tourism, Gol, proposed
(August 2022 and November 2022) to forfeit the final instalment of the project,
as the UT had not submitted the operation and maintenance details and
100 per cent Utilisation Certificate (UC) for the funds released. The Gol further
advised to fix responsibility on the specific officer for non-compliance.

As per the final UC submitted (February 2024) by GoP to Gol, the expenditure
incurred for the ‘Development of Pathways and Ghat along Godavari River in
Yanam’ was ® 2.76 crore (including tender advertisement charges). Thus, the

76


Chapter II — Compliance Audit

failure on the part of PWD led to lost opportunity to avail Gol funds to the tune
of रहें 2.31 crore (work awarded cost 2 5.07 crore - expenditure incurred
% 2.76 crore), resulting in non-completion of the project and non-achievement
of the intended objective.

The Government replied (November 2025) that the PWD, Yanam had reported
certain technical constraints and alignment overlap with proposed flood
protection works and the foreclosure decision was taken by the PWD, Yanam
based on technical considerations. Hence, Tourism Department is not in a
position to offer independent justification on the issue or promises for
completion of remaining project work.

The reply is not acceptable as Department of Tourism, being an implementing
agency, did not review the foreclosure decisions taken by the PWD through
their technical officials. Thus, failure of the PWD to complete the project on
time and failure of the Tourism Department to monitor the implementation of
the project led to non-availing of Gol funds to complete the remaining length of
pathways from chainage 110 to 400 meters and the provision of greenery,
granite benches, and illuminations.

Recommendation:

° The Government should ensure timely completion of sanctioned projects
in future and take all necessary steps to complete the present project so
that the benefits of the work already executed are realised.

RH? Audit Planning

The unit offices under various Departments are categorised into A (high),
B (medium) and C (low) units according to their revenue position, past trends
of audit observations, nature/volume of transactions, etc. The Annual Audit
Plan is prepared based on the risk analysis which, inter-alia, includes statistical
analysis of the revenue earnings during the past five years, features of the tax
administration, audit coverage and its impact during the past five years, etc.

of which,7? units were planned and audited during the year 2023-24, i.e.,
17.50 per cent of the total auditable units.

Response of the Depar overnment to

331 Inspection Reports

Principal Accountant General (Audit-II), Tamil Nadu and Puducherry arrange
periodical inspection of the Government Departments to test check the
transactions and verify the maintenance of important accounts and other records
as per the prescribed rules and procedures. These inspections are followed up
with Inspection Reports (IRs) incorporating irregularities detected during the
inspection and not settled on the spot, which are issued to the Heads of offices
inspected with copies to the next higher authorities for taking corrective action.
The Heads of offices/Government are required to comply with the observations
contained in the IRs, rectify the defects and omissions promptly and report
compliance to the office of the Accountant General within one month from the
dates of issue of the IRs. Serious financial irregularities are also brought to the
notice of the Heads of Departments and the Government by the office of the
Accountant General.

This Chapter contains one Subject Specific Compliance Audit on E-Way Bill
System under Goods and Services Tax and three draft paragraphs on

under Goods

37,1 Introduction

RL E-Way Bill System

Goods and Services Tax (GST) has been introduced with effect from
01 July 2017, subsuming a wide range of Indirect Taxes based on the paradigm
of ‘One Nation One Tax’.

E-Way Bill (EWB) is conceived as a shift from Government-monitored tax
administration model to a self-reporting model by the taxpayer. The key
objective of introduction of EWB is to safeguard Government revenue. EWB
is a document required for the movement of goods and is designed to capture
details of goods being moved. The EWB was introduced in the UT of
Puducherry with effect from 01 April 2018 for movement of goods in intra-state
or inter-state, for a value exceeding = 50,000.

Rules 138 and 138 (A to E) of Puducherry Goods and Services Tax Rules
(PGST Rules), 2017 provides for the EWB mechanism. The information on the
consignment is to be furnished in EWB portal prior to movement of goods.

84


Chapter IIT - Revenue Receipts

करके Organisational structure

Commercial Taxes Department, Puducherry (CTD), working under the aegis of
the Finance Department, Government of Puducherry is the apex body
implementing GST for Puducherry State administered taxpayers. The CTD is
headed by Commissioner (State Taxes). There are seven Divisions under
Puducherry CTD each headed by a Commercial Tax Officer (CTO).

EWBs generated for outward supplies are supported by invoices and the invoice
details are required to be reported in the GSTR-1 by the regular taxpayers and
in GSTR-4/CMP-08 for composition taxpayers. As per the instructions?
provided by the department, the Proper Officer! (PO) may rely upon the
data/details available in EWB Portal at the time of scrutiny of returns.

37-13 Information Systems used for EWBs

The EWB common portal is managed by National Informatics Centre. In
March 2020, EWB portal had been integrated with the VAHAN’* system of the
Ministry of Road Transport and Highways, so that vehicle registration number
can be verified at the time of generating EWB. FASTag system had been
integrated with the EWB system with effect from 01 January 2021.

The POs (both Centre and State/UT) can access the EWB Portal by - (i) web
browser (ii) GST EWB mobile app.

हा Processes involved in EWB System

The EWB system includes various processes viz., the enrolment in the portal,
generation of EWB, extension of validity, cancellation and rejection of the
EWBs already generated, etc. The entire process flow of EWB system under
GST is depicted in the following flowchart (Figure 3.1).

Para 6.1 of Standard Operating Procedure (SoP) for Scrutiny of GST returns issued
(March 2022) by CBIC and adopted by Puducherry CTD in May 2022.

VAHAN is a highly flexible and comprehensive system that takes care of all the
burdensome activities of Vehicle Registration, leaving the Transport Department to deal
with more important business issues.

The process and important terminologies used in the EWB System are discussed
in the following paragraphs:

86


Chapter III - Revenue Receipts

(a) Enrolment in the Portal

For generation of EWBs, GST registered persons, transporters and citizens are
required to enroll in EWB portal by providing required details.

(b) Generation of EWBs

As per Rule 138 of the PGST Rules, EWB can be generated electronically by
furnishing details of the consignments in Part A of the form GST EWB-01 by
the consignor, transporter or the consignee.

In Part B of EWB-01, vehicle number for consignment by road and transport
document number are provided for intra-state movement of goods in excess of
50 km and inter-state movement of goods. In cases when the goods are
purchased by the buyer and transported from the place of supplier to the place
of recipient on behalf of the buyer, EWB can be generated either by the supplier
or by the buyer.

(० Validity of EWBs

As per Rule 138(10) of the PGST Rules, the validity of the EWB depends upon
the distance and the type of shipment. The validity period is one day for
200 km in respect of consignment other than over dimensional cargo and one
additional day for every 200 km or part thereof with effect from 01 January 2021.

(d) Extension of EWBs
As per Provision-3 of the Rule 138(10) of the PGST Rules, the validity of the

EWB can be extended by the transporter after updating the details in Part B of
form GST ४५४७-01, within eight hours from the time of its expiry.

(fe Cancellation of EWBs

As per Rule 138(9) of the PGST Rules EWBs generated and not verified
in-transit may be cancelled electronically on the common portal within 24 hours
of generation.

oO Rejection of EWBs

As per Rule 138(11) and (12) of the PGST Rules, the details of the EWB
generated will be communicated electronically to the recipient and he is
required to communicate his acceptance or rejection of the goods being
transported.

3715 Audit objectives

The audit objectives of this Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) were to
examine:

> Whether EWB mechanism was functioning in protecting revenue
interest of the Government; and

> Whether the preventive/enforcement activities of the Department in
enforcing EWB mechanism were efficient and effective.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

3.7.1.6 Audit criteria

The SSCA on EWB System under GST had been evaluated against the
following audit criteria:

> Puducherry Goods and Services Tax Act, 2017 (PGST Act), as amended

> Puducherry Goods and Services Tax Rules, 2017 (PGST Rules), as
amended

> Integrated Goods and Services Tax (IGST) Act and Rules, 2017, as
amended

> Notifications/Circulars/Instructions issued by Central Board of Indirect
Taxes and Customs (CBIC)/NIC and CTD.

RLF Audit scope

EWB transactions of the persons falling under Puducherry State jurisdiction
pertaining to the period between 01 April 2018 and 31 March 2023 were
covered in this SSCA. These persons were selected based on EWBs relating to
the period between 01 April 2018 and 31 March 2022. Audit examined the
functioning of EWB system under the GST regime with reference to the audit
objectives. EWB data for the period from 01 April 2018 to 31 March 2023 was
extracted from Goods and Services Tax Network (GSTN) and analysed. The
audit was restricted EWB transactions relating to movement of goods by
roadways and movement of goods through railway/airway/seaway were not
covered.

The scope of audit also covered evaluation of the preventive/enforcement
functions of the Department related to EWBs, viz., interception of vehicles,
verification of goods movement documents and inspection for the period
between 01 April 2018 and 31 March 2022.

उ.7.1.68 Audit methodology

A risk-based approach had been attempted for this SSCA as EWB generation is
necessary to cause any movement of goods subject to the threshold limit. Audit
samples for Audit objective-1 were evolved based on the identified Key
Problem Areas (KPAs) which are listed in Appendix 3.1.

Audit selected 37 taxpayers for conducting substantive audit® through data
analysis. Audit was conducted between May 2024 and April 2025 by examining
records relating to the selected 37 taxpayers. This included EWB data in respect
of these 37 taxpayers for the audit period which was obtained from the EWB
MIS system. Similarly, invoice-wise GSTR-1 data and GSTR-2A/2B data was

Detailed verification of transactions covered by EWBs with reference to returns filed
by taxpayers and their books of accounts to ascertain the extent of tax compliance.

Chapter III - Revenue Receipts

obtained from NIC-eGST’ portal. This data was analysed using IDEA*. EWBs
related to these 37 taxpayers were analysed with reference to invoice wise
GSTR-1 data, GST returns and other records available in the BO-Web’
Application.

The second Audit objective evaluated the preventive/enforcement activities viz.,
operational preparedness, effectiveness of anti-evasion measures and intra-
department co-ordination, related to EWB. The enforcement activities in the
UT are being performed by the Enforcement wing and the seven divisions
amongst other functions.

For analysing preventive function relating to EWBs, it was planned to scrutinise
asample of 50 cases. Audit requisitioned the information on EWB verifications
and booked cases from CTD, Puducherry. Since the department did not provide
the required details till the end of March 2025, 40 high value cases were selected
on judgemental sampling based on EWB-03 Part B downloaded from the
departmental" portal.

An Entry Conference was conducted with the Commissioner of Commercial

methodology were discussed. An Exit Conference was conducted with the
Secretary to the Government (State Taxes), Puducherry on 7 August 2025. The
replies received from the Government on the audit observations have suitably
been incorporated.

We also acknowledge the co-operation extended by the CTD, Puducherry in
providing many of the necessary records for the smooth conduct of audit.

BALI Scope limitation

The documents/information related to cancelled EWBs, time extended EWBs
and EWBs rejected by the recipient were not produced to audit.

Further, neither the details of booked cases (EWB-03 (Part A and B)) nor the
related files were provided despite reminders. In the absence of details of cases
booked the operational preparedness to assess adequacy and effectiveness of
preventive function in examination of EWB during transit also could not be
ensured in audit as the details relating to adequacy of manpower, availability of
patrolling vehicles and conduct of inspections were not furnished to audit.

3:73 Results of Audit

The SSCA on EWB was conducted based on KPAs identified through data
analysis of sampled EWBs and detailed verification of related records for the
years 2018-19 to 2022-23.

Audit analysed the EWBs pertaining to the selected 37 taxpayers over the period
of five years vis-d-vis GST returns'! and other records!? available in BO-Web
application. The deficiencies noticed in detailed scrutiny of sampled cases were
issued to the Department between September 2024 and April 2025.

The deficiencies noticed are discussed in detail in the following paragraphs:
S731 Non/short discharge of tax liability

(a) Composition Levy Scheme

Section 10(2)(c) of the PGST Act imposes a restriction that the taxpayer shall
not be eligible to opt for composition levy scheme if he is engaged in making
any inter-st inter-state ate outward supplies of goods. Rule 6(2) of the PGST
Rules provides that the composition taxpayer shall be liable to pay tax under
regular rate from the day he ceases to satisfy the condition.

Based on data analysis and substantive audit, it was observed that three out of
five taxpayers, who opted for composition scheme generated six EWBs, in
which inter-State outward supplies were made. These taxpayers were required
to be brought out of the scheme and were liable to pay tax at normal rate with
effect from the date of effecting inter-State supply (Appendix 3.2).

On this being pointed out (October 2024), the Government stated (July 2025)
that an amount of = 0.12 lakh was collected in one case, and ASMT-10 notice
was issued in one case and in third case the Government forwarded the Proper
Officer’s reply (June 2025), where in, it was stated that the taxpayer accepted
the generation of EWBs.

(b) Non/short-discharge of tax liability by the taxpayers

Under Section 61, returns filed by the taxpayers must be scrutinised by the PO
to verify the correctness of the returns and suitable action had to be taken.

(0) Non-discharge of tax liability due to Non-filing of returns

Audit observed that one taxpayer (GSTIN - 34**********] 71) under Goods
Division-III, had effected outward supplies of = 2.07 crore through 85 EWBs,
involving tax of = 0.24 crore during January 2019 to July 2019. The taxpayer

GSTR-1(Monthly return declaring outward supplies), GSTR-3B (Monthly summary
return of outward supplies, ITC claimed and payment of tax), GSTR-9 (Annual return),
(५०-08 (Quarterly return by Composition Taxpayers) and GSTR-2A/2B (A System-
generated statement of inward supplies).

a Annual financial statements, stock accounts, purchase/sales registers.

91


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

disclosed outward supplies effected through 26 EWBs out of total 85 EWBs in
their GSTR-1 for this period. It was further observed that the tax liability was
also not discharged as GSTR-3B was not filed.

Declaring outward supplies in GSTR-1 without discharging the tax liability is
fraught with the potential risk of passing on of ITC without payment of tax. The
ITC involved in these 26 EWBs was @ 0.08 crore.

On this being pointed out (October 2024), the Government, while
acknowledging the lapses highlighted by audit, stated (July 2025) that notices
in ASMT-10 were issued (June 2025) to the taxpayer.

(ii) Non-discharge of tax liability by the taxpayers who filed ‘nil’
returns

Audit observed that during the period from April 2018 to March 2022, two out
of 32 taxpayers effected outward supplies, by generating 477 EWBs, amounting
to हें 6.34 crore involving tax of = 1.07 crore. The taxpayers under-reported the
transaction effected through EWBs in GSTR-1 and filed ‘nil’ GSTR-3B
resulting in non-discharge of the tax liability. The under reporting of supplies
had a potential risk of passing on ITC of = 0.98 crore without payment of tax to
the recipients. The amount of tax recoverable worked out to = 1.07 crore
(Appendix 3.3).

On this being pointed out (October 2024), the Government replied (July 2025)
that in one taxpayer was under National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT) and
hence no action could be taken. The reply is not tenable as the Department
failed to identify the observation despite the taxpayer being selected for scrutiny
of returns for the period of 2018-19. Had this been identified earlier, the
Government dues could have been recovered through NCLT, as they were the
first charge. In another case, the Government issued intimation of tax
ascertained under DRC-01A" (June 2025).

Recommendation:
° Department may consider issuing suitable instructions to the POs for
red flagging EWBs generated by the taxpayers, who did not file

mandatory returns to initiate suitable action to recover the dues where
applicable.
(० Generation of EWBs by cancelled taxpayers due to
retrospective cancellation

As per Section 63 of the PGST Act, in respect of a taxable person whose
registration had been cancelled but remains liable to pay tax, the PO may

Intimation of Tax Ascertained Payable.

92


Chapter IIT - Revenue Receipts

proceed to assess the tax liability of such taxable person to the best of his
judgement.

Audit observed that five out of 12 taxpayers generated 23 EWBs for effecting
outward supplies of = 1.46 crore during the period from April 2018 to
March 2021 after the effective date of cancellation as the cancellations were
effected retrospectively by the Department.

It was further observed that these taxpayers also failed to discharge the potential
tax liability of = 0.26 crore for these EWBs (Appendix 3.4).

On this being pointed out (October 2024), the Government stated (July 2025)
that in case of two taxpayers, a demand has been raised in DRC-07"
(June 2025). DRC-01A was issued in one case and in the fourth case, an amount
of = 0.12 lakh was collected. And in the remaining case, the Government stated
that an enquiry would be conducted.

It is pertinent to mention that the PO failed to assess the taxpayers’ tax liability
while cancelling the registration.

Recommendation:

. Department may consider issuing suitable instructions to the POs for
considering EWBs generated by the taxpayer before cancellation of
registration retrospectively and to take action for recovery of tax,
wherever applicable.

(व) Taxpayers generating multiple EWBs for single invoice

As per Para 5-1 of the user manual issued by the NIC, the taxpayer while
generating the EWB is required to enter the document number relating to the
consignment. Invoice number is the document number in respect of
consignments relating to supplies and it should be unique. Hence, only one
EWB is required to be generated based on each invoice.

Audit observed that seven out of 32 taxpayers, had generated 9,092 EWBs,
involving 4,003 unique invoices of an assessable value of = 120.22 crore with a
tax liability of = 32.94 crore.

Audit also observed that multiple invoices were used in generating these EWBs
which recorded different outward dates, vehicle numbers and destinations.

Generation of multiple EWBs based on single invoice indicated lack of
validation in EWB common portal to prevent generation of multiple EWBs.

Considering the invoices which appeared more than once there was a potential
risk of under reporting of assessable value of % 64.60 crore and consequential
short discharge of tax liability upto = 17.73 crore (Appendix 3.5).

Summary of order.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

On this being pointed out (October 2024), the Government stated (July 2025)
that notice/order were issued in four cases. In one case, the Government stated
that a demand had been raised in DRC-07.

In one case, the Government stated that the taxpayer was under NCLT and hence

returns for the period of 2018-19. Had this been identified earlier, the

discrepancy as the EWB referred was invalid and did not relate to the taxpayer,
audit, however, found that the EWB was valid and related to that particular
taxpayer.

Recommendation:

. Department may consider including suitable input controls in the EWB
System to prevent generation of multiple EWBs for the same invoice.

(e) Discharge of tax liability by the taxpayers with respect to
other KPAs

Section 37 of the PGST Act, 2017, read with Rules 59(1) of PGST Rules, 2017,
specifies that all outward supplies shall be reported in form GSTR-1.
Section 39(1) and Section 44(1) of the PGST Act, 2017, specifies that all inward
and outward supplies, input tax credit availed, tax payable, tax paid shall be
reported in GSTR-3B return, for every calendar month and in GSTR-9 return
for the financial year respectively.

Rule 138(1) of the PGST Rule, 2017, specifies that information, including HSN
code, relating to the Goods, whose consignment value exceeds the prescribed
limit shall be furnished in Part-A GST-EWB-01 in common portal.

(0) Non-discharge of tax liability by taxpayers who generated
EWBs without reporting in GSTR-1

Audit observed that during the period from April 2018 to March 2023, seven

out of 32 taxpayers effected outward supplies by generating 9,852 EWBs,

amounting to = 295.17 crore involving tax of = 50.07 crore, but failed to report

the supplies in GSTR-1. The amount of non/short payment of tax worked out

to = 50.07 crore (Appendix 3.6).

In addition, audit observed that four out of these seven taxpayers further
generated 10,650 EWBs amounting to = 385.46 crore but the tax amount was
shown as zero. However, the HSN code featured in these EWBs did not pertain
to commodities whose prescribed tax rate were not nil/zero (Appendix 3.7). It
was observed that transactions effected through these EWBs were also not
reported in GSTR-1.

(ii) Undischarged tax liability resulting in short payment of tax

revealed that the taxpayers did not discharge the tax liability of = 1.26 crore

with the declared tax liability in GSTR-1. The total tax payable as per HSN

resulting in short declaration of tax of = 4.74 crore (Appendix 3.9).

On this being pointed out (November 2024), the Government stated (July 2025)
that notices have been issued in all three cases.

(g) Discrepancies noticed in availing of ITC

As per Section 122, where a taxable person who transports any goods without
EWB, he shall be liable to pay a penalty of = 10,000 for the UT of Puducherry
and similar amount to Government of India.

Audit observed that during the period from April 2018 to March 2023, 16 out

taxpayers in excess of the prescribed limits in GSTR-1 for = 159.98 crore,
involving tax of = 24.56 crore. However, it was observed that the taxpayer
failed to generate EWBs for the same. This is fraught with a potential risk of
passing on of ITC without actual movement of supplies. The total penalty
leviable for non-generation of EWBs worked out to 2 5.59 crore
(Appendix 3.10).

stated (July 2025) that ASMT 10/Notices were issued in ten cases. In one of

failed to identify the observation despite the taxpayer being selected for scrutiny
of returns for the period of 2018-19. In remaining one case, Government
forwarded the PO’s reply (January 2025), in which it was stated that the
taxpayer had not generated EWBs and hence penalty under Section 122 of
PGST Act needs to be collected.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

(ii) Mismatch of ITC between GSTR-3B and GSTR-2A/2B

As per Section 16(1) of the PGST Act, every registered person shall be entitled
to take ITC charged on any supply of goods or services or both to him, which
are used or intended to be used in the course or furtherance of his business and
the said amount shall be credited to the electronic credit ledger of such person.

The claim of ITC as per GSTR-3B was correlated with taxable inward supplies,
auto-populated in GSTR-2A/2B in respect of 32 taxpayers selected for audit and
observed that eight taxpayers had availed = 1,831.39 crore as ITC whereas the
taxable inward supplies available under GSTR-2A/2B_ was only
2 1,690.18 crore. This mismatch of = 141.21 crore had a potential risk of excess
availing of ITC by the recipients (Appendix 3.11).

On this being pointed out (between October 2024 and January 2025), the
Government stated (July 2025) that, DRC-01A was issued in one case and
ASMT-10/Notices were issued in seven cases. However, audit noticed that out
of seven cases, where ASMT-10/Notices were stated to be issued, five did not
relate to the issue pointed out by audit. This was also reiterated in the Exit
Conference.

(iii) Non-reversal of ITC on exempt supplies

As per Section 17(2) of the PGST Act, if taxable supplies are used partly for
effecting taxable supplies and partly for exempted supplies, ITC shall be
restricted to taxable supplies only and ITC attributable to exempted supplies
shall be reversed.

Audit observed that five out of 32 taxpayers effected both taxable and exempted
supplies during the period April 2020 to March 2023. Audit noticed that these
five taxpayers availed ITC of ¢ 15.98 crores, which included ITC of रं 0.31 crore
pertaining to exempted supplies. However, the ITC pertaining to exempted
supplies were not reversed (Appendix 3.12).

On this being pointed out (December 2024), the Government stated that
(July 2025) ASMT 10/Notices were issued in three cases and % 0.01 crore was
recovered in one case. In another case, Government, in its reply, stated that it
was due to wrong entry in Column 3.1(c) (Nil rated, exempted) instead of
Col 3.1(b) (zero rated). Since all sales were made to SEZ customers there was
no need to reverse ITC under Rule 42 and 43. However, the audit’s observation
was about reversal due to non-GST supplies in column 3.1(e) of GSTR-3B in
2021-22 and not about SEZ supplies.

(iv) Availing of ITC without generating EWBs

As per Section 16(2) of the PGST Act, ITC is not eligible unless the taxpayer
had received the goods or services or both.

Chapter III - Revenue Receipts

During scrutiny of GSTR-2A/2B data, audit observed that in case of four out of
32 taxpayers there was a receipt of taxable supplies of = 39.49 crore and they
had claimed ITC of ₹ 5.77 crore in GSTR-3B during the period April 2019 to
March 2023. Further data analysis revealed that EWBs were not generated in

availing of ITC by taxpayers without actual movement of supplies
(Appendix 3.13).

to be issued, the notice, however, did not relate to the audit observation.

(h) Other points of interest - Wilful suppression of turnover by a
group of taxpayers

Audit identified, through data analysis, a group of 26 taxpayers Pan India,
making consignments of goods without discharging tax liabilities. These
taxpayers mainly dealt with tobacco products, copper and nuts. These goods
were sent to various States. Audit noticed that two taxpayers falling under
Goods Division-I, CTD, Puducherry were part of this group. Audit verified
inward and outward EWBs along with the GST returns pertaining to these two
taxpayers and the following observations were made:

(0) Non-discharge of tax liability

Section 39 read with Rule 61 stipulates that taxpayers are required to furnish a
return in GSTR-3B declaring the details of inward and outward supplies of
goods or services or both, ITC availed, tax payable, tax paid.

Audit scrutiny revealed that these two taxpayers generated 348 EWBs in respect
of outward supplies valued at = 57.77 crore during the period from April 2019
to May 2019 and March 2020 to July 2020. Tax including cess involved in
respect of these outward supplies was % 6.37 crore. The taxpayers, failed to
report transactions in GSTR-1 and did not file GSTR-3B which led to non-
discharge of tax liability of  6.37crore (Appendix 3.14).

On this being pointed out (September 2023, October 2024 and November 2024),
the Government stated (July 2025) that in both cases intimation of tax
ascertained in Form DRC-01A were issued.

(ii) Movement of prohibited products through EWBs

Audit further noticed that the major commodities received in Puducherry from
Delhi and Tamil Nadu by one taxpayer were tobacco related products. It is
pertinent to mention that Gutkha, Pan-Masala and any other food products
containing tobacco are banned in Tamil Nadu and Puducherry under the Food
Safety and Standards Act (FSSA), 2006. This indicates that there was no

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

mechanism to prevent transportation of banned goods in EWB system. On this
being pointed out (September 2023, October 2024, November 2024), the
Government replied (July 2025) that it would take appropriate steps to address
the gap highlighted.

Recommendation:

e Department may incorporate validation control in the EWB system to
alert taxpayers whenever EWB is generated for movement of banned
goods.

3.7.4 Preventive functions of the Department

The PO may authorise any officer to intercept any conveyance to verify the
EWB. The CTD, Puducherry issued instructions elaborating the procedure for
interception of conveyances for inspection of goods in movement, detention,
release and confiscation of goods and conveyances for contravening the
provisions of Act/Rules relating to EWB. A summary report of every inspection
of goods in transit shall be recorded online by the PO in Part A of
Form EWB-03 within 24 hours of inspection and the final report in Part B of
Form EWB-03 within three days of such inspection.

In CTD, Puducherry, the responsibility of performing the enforcement functions
relating to the verification of EWBs was entrusted to the officers of the
enforcement wing and the divisions along with their regular activities.

3.7.4.1 Efficiency and effectiveness of the preventive/enforcement
activities of the Department in enforcing EWB provisions

Audit examined EWB related functions of CTD, Puducherry with specific focus
on (i) operational preparedness; (ii) effectiveness of anti-evasion measures; and
(iii) intra-departmental co-ordination in monitoring EWB transactions. During
verification, audit noticed deficiencies in the activities as detailed in the
subsequent paragraphs:

As per sub-section (3) of Section 68 of the PGST Act during inspection the
transporter shall be liable to produce the documents and also allow the
inspection of goods.

Section 129 of the PGST Act provides for detention and seizure of goods where
any person transports any goods in contravention of the provisions of this Act
of the Rules.

CTD, Puducherry adopted CBIC instructions, which detailed procedure for
interception of conveyances for inspection of goods and action to be taken by
the POs. Scrutiny of records revealed the following:

100


Chapter IIT - Revenue Receipts

(a) Operational Preparedness

(9 Dedicated unit

A dedicated unit for EWB related enforcement activities like verification of
EWBs during interception of vehicles and follow-up action, wherever required,
utilising EWB analytical reports in planning the EWB verification, etc., can
improve the efficiency of preventive functions. Audit ascertained that no
dedicated unit was formed for conducting EWB related enforcement activities
and the Divisional Offices and Enforcement Wing were entrusted with the
verification of EWB functions.

On being pointed out by audit (May 2024), the Government replied (July 2025)
the suggestion of audit to form a dedicated unit for EWB enforcement was well
taken and would be duly considered in future.

(ii) Adequacy of manpower

Details of existing manpower assigned with EWB verification functions was
requisitioned from the Department to ascertain whether the manpower was
adequate, however, the CTD, Puducherry did not furnish information despite
reminders.

On this being pointed out (May 2024), the Government replied (July 2025) that
the details were already furnished to audit and the copy of the same is again
being furnished. However, the details were not furnished to audit.

(iii) Analytical Reports on EWBs

NIC generates about 100 analytical reports on EWB transactions and shares the
same through the NIC-GST EWB analytical system portal with the GST
Departments under Centre and State formations for their use. The manual on
the comprehensive analytics on EWB issued by the NIC provides the nature of
each report and its usage. These reports help the POs in identifying cases of
fake invoices, tax evasion, recycling etc., by using the vehicle movement
information.

Access and use of Analytical Reports

Audit ascertained from the J3 report (usage of analytical reports by Officers)
that only five users of the Department were given access to the NIC GST EWB
analytical system.

It was noticed that, however, no officer who had been given access to the
analytical report viewed the same during the period from April 2018 to
March 2022. When audit requested (January 2025) that the MIS reports in
NICeGST portal used for EWB verification may be shared, the Department did
not provide them despite reminders.

101


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

On being pointed out (January 2025), the Government replied (July 2025) that
various reports like Red flag reports and BIFA Reports etc., were recently
available and are being made use of the POs. However, the fact remains that
the number of officers who viewed analytical reports during the period from
2018 to 2022 was ‘nil’. This was also reiterated during the Exit Conference.

Though NIC-GST EWB analytical system portal generated analytical reports,
non-usage of these reports by the field formations indicated weak monitoring
mechanism on transactions related to EWBs.

(iv) MIS Reports on EWBs

EWB-MIS System, which is available under NIC-GST Analytical System
provides several MIS reports such as RFID vehicle related report on live
vigilance for EWB, EWB-03-Part-A, Part-B, Verification Report for EWBs,
EWB-03 correction list, e-Invoice Non-compliance etc.

Access and use of EWB MIS reports

Audit ascertained from the J3 report (usage of Reports by Officers) that 19 users
of the Department were given access to the NIC EWB MIS System. It was seen
that no officer who had been given access to the MIS report accessed the portal
during the period from April 2018 to March 2022. Non-usage of these
EWB-MIS reports by the field formations indicated inadequate monitoring
mechanism in verification of EWBs.

On being pointed out (January 2025), the Government replied (July 2025) that
several enhanced and integrated reporting tools such as Red Flag Reports and
BIFA Reports had been used by POs. However, the Government further stated
that steps were being considered to reinforce oversight and improve utilisation
of these resources across field formations.

Recommendation:

° Government may issue suitable instructions for utilising the Analytical
and MIS reports on EWBs for effective monitoring to verify compliance
of EWB provisions by the taxpayers.

According to Rule 138 of the PGST Rules, in case of transportation of goods,
an EWB is required to be generated before the commencement of movement of
the consignment. For conducting verification of EWBs, the CTD, Puducherry
had adopted CBIC issued instructions where procedure for interception of
conveyances for inspection of goods and action to be taken by the POs.

EWB-MIS under NIC-GST Analytical System portal. A total of 40 high value

booked cases were randomly selected based on EWB-03 Part B downloaded

(0) Non/short creation of demand for tax and penalty during
EWB verification

As per Section 129(1) of PGST Act, when the goods and vehicle are detained
for any contravention of the provisions of the Act, then the same shall be
released on payment of the applicable tax and equal amount of penalty upto
31 December 2021. With effect from 01 January 2022, penalty at 200 per cent
of the applicable tax was required to be collected apart from tax under
Section 9 of the PGST Act.

According to the Guidelines issued by the CBIC and adopted by CTD,
Puducherry the PO shall upload demand order (MOV-09) on the common
portal.

Intrastate movements

During scrutiny it was observed that there was a deficiency in nine'® out of
10 intrastate cases booked where the consignor as well as consignee were from
Puducherry.

In five of nine cases it was noticed that tax and penalty levied was not in

In addition, it was observed that MOV-09 was not available on the EWB portal
for seven out of nine cases resulting in demand not being created.

Further, it was observed that the intercepting officer revised the assessable value
and tax value during inspection in four out of nine cases. However, it was

Section 9.

In the absence of creation of demand in the portal, the PO of the consignor

Details of these nine cases are given in Appendix 3.15.

105


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

3:75 Conclusion and Recommendations

हक Conclusion

The Subject Specific Compliance Audit of EWB System was undertaken with
the objective of verifying whether EWB mechanism was functioning in
protecting revenue interest of the Government; and whether the
preventive/enforcement activities of the Department in enforcing EWB
mechanism were efficient and effective.

Audit observed certain deficiencies in the EWB system as it allowed generation
of EWBs by nil filers and non-filers of GST returns, by cancelled taxpayers and
by composition taxpayers making interstate supplies. The system also allowed
generation of multiple EWBs for single invoices, payment of taxes in deviation
of applicable tax rates to the HSN codes declared in EWBs. These deficiencies
had a consequential impact of non/short discharge of tax liability and potential
risk of passing on of ineligible ITC. Audit also observed cases where EWBs
were generated but the transactions were not reported in GSTR-1 returns;
transactions were reported in GSTR-1 without generating EWBs; transactions
were reported in GSTR-1 without payment of tax in GSTR-3B; taxpayers
availing ITC without inwards EWBs and movement of prohibited goods
through EWBs.

Further, the operational preparedness of the Department for enforcement
functions could be further strengthened and deficiencies relating to
non-utilisation of available resources viz., analytical reports and MIS reports
were noticed. Audit also observed that Proper Officers were not following the
due process in assessment and collection of Government dues during
interception of vehicles.

Audit also encountered scope limitation due to non-production of records/data
pertaining to patrolling of vehicles, manpower and conduct of inspection etc.

3.7.5.2 Recommendations

. Department may consider issuing suitable instructions to the POs for
red flagging EWBs generated by the taxpayers, who did not file
mandatory returns to initiate suitable action to recover the dues where
applicable.

. Department may consider issuing suitable instructions to the POs for
considering EWBs generated by the taxpayer before cancellation of
registration retrospectively and to take action for recovery of tax,
wherever applicable.

. Department may consider including suitable input controls in the EWB

3.8.1 Short collection of Motor Vehicle tax

Short collection of Motor Vehicle tax and penalty for heavy passenger
motor vehicles with contract carriage permits amounting to = 14.30 crore.

As per Section 74(1) of The Motor Vehicles Act, 1988, Regional Transport
Authority may, on an application made to it under Section 73, grant a contract
carriage permit in accordance with the application or with such modifications
as it deems fit or refuse to grant such a permit.

As per Section 3 of Puducherry Motor Vehicles Taxation Act, 1967, motor
vehicle tax is leviable on every motor vehicle (used or kept for use in the Union
Territory of Puducherry) at the rates prescribed as per Schedule-I of the Act.
Section 6-A of the Act stipulates that the tax due shall be paid within such a
period, not being less than seven days or more than thirty days from the
commencement of the quarter, half-year or year, as may be prescribed.

In respect of heavy passenger motor vehicles, which are considered as ‘contract
carriage’, the quarterly motor vehicle tax payable per vehicle, having seating
capacity not exceeding 54 persons including the driver, is = 1,200 per seat or
@ 1,500 per sleeper or berth for every person other than the driver.

Section 9 of the Act stipulates that whenever the registered owner or any person
who has possession or control of any motor vehicle used or kept for use in

107



Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

Puducherry defaults in making payment of the tax, the licensing officer may
direct that, in addition to the amount of arrears, a sum not exceeding the annual
tax payable shall be recovered from him by way of penalty.

In this connection, the Government of UT of Puducherry has issued
(November 2010) orders to impose penalty along with tax from the defaulters
as detailed in the Table 3.11.

vehicles with contract carriage permits did not pay tax amounting to 2 7.15 crore
for periods between April 2019 and March 2024" (Appendix 3.17). Besides
the tax, the penalty leviable at 100 per cent for non-payment of tax worked out
to 27.15 crore.

the toll on 126 occasions.

Thus, failure of the Department to ensure timely collection of motor vehicle tax
from the defaulters had resulted in short realisation of tax and penalty of
र₹ 14.30 crore” in respect of 140 vehicles. Regular movement of vehicles
without payment of motor vehicle taxes indicated the absence of monitoring
mechanism to identify vehicles plying on the road without paying mandated
taxes to the Government.
