3.8.2 Short levy of Stamp duty

Short collection of stamp duty and registration fees of = 36.90 lakh due
to misclassification of conveyance deeds as cancellation deeds.

As per Section 2(10) of Indian Stamp (IS) Act 1899, "Conveyance" includes a
conveyance on sale and every instrument by which property, whether movable
or immovable, is transferred inter vivo and which is not otherwise specifically
provided for by Schedule I.

As per Article 23, in the case of conveyance of immovable property, stamp duty
is leviable at the rate of 10 per cent and registration fees of 0.5 per cent on the
sale value of the property or the Guideline Register Value, whichever is higher
is required to be collected. As per Article 17 of the Schedule I of the Indian
Stamp Act (IS Act), for instruments of cancellation, if attested and not otherwise
provided for, stamp duty of % 50 is to be levied on the same.

It was judicially held?! that there can be no such thing as cancellation of
conveyance under which right of property had already been passed. Property
can be retransferred only by way of re-conveyance. Further, it was held”? by the
Hon’ble Madras High Court that where a transfer is effected by way of sale with
the condition that title will pass on payment of consideration, then such
instrument or sale can be cancelled by a deed of cancellation with the consent
of both the parties on the ground of non-payment of consideration.

As per Note 9 under Section 3 of the IS Act, any material alteration made with
the consent of parties in an instrument after it is complete, renders a new stamp
necessary. An alteration is considered material when it changes the legal effect
of the instrument i.e., when it makes any material changes as regards the rights
and liabilities of parties or as regards their legal position.

Scrutiny of records revealed that sale deed, release deed, settlement deed and
gift deed executed earlier were cancelled subsequently, treating them as

a cf Emperror Vs Rameshardoss 32 811 171 SIC 697.
ad W.A Nos 592 & 938 of 2009, in Latif Estate Line India Ltd., Vs Registration
Department.

109


Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

cancellation deed under Article 17 instead of conveyance deed as stipulated in
Article 23 of Schedule I of IS Act, as discussed in the subsequent paragraphs.

> Scrutiny of records of six District Registrar/Sub-Registrar Offices of
Puducherry revealed that conveyance of property effected (between
2010 and 2023) through sale deeds on receipt of consideration was
cancelled through instruments registered subsequently (between
2018 and 2023) justifying with reasons viz., possession not handed over,
to make alternate arrangement, etc. Since the sellers had reacquired the
right and interest over the properties from the original purchaser through
cancellation deeds, these deeds were to be treated as conveyance deeds.
This resulted in short collection of stamp duty and registration fees of
= 9.05 lakh (Appendix 3.18).

id In the Sub-Registrar Offices, Oulgaret, Bahour and Villianur,
six settlement deeds executed (between 2012 and 2020) were cancelled
between 2021 and 2024 through instrument of cancellation instead of
conveyance. This resulted in short collection of stamp duty and
registration fees of = 9.68 lakh (Appendix 3.19).

>> One release deed executed in District Registrar Office, Puducherry on
15 February 2017 (Document No. 2354) was subsequently cancelled on
5 October 2022 (Document No. 31952). Since ownership had already
been transferred, this should have been treated as conveyance deed.
Accordingly, the stamp duty and registration fee payable, treating it as
conveyance deed, worked out to = 10.84 lakh (Appendix 3.20).

> In the Sub-Registrar Office, Yanam, two gift deeds executed (Document
No. 813 and 814) in 2006 were subsequently cancelled in 2019 after
12 years. The stamp duty and registration fee payable, treating them as
conveyance deed, worked out to = 7.33 lakh (Appendix 3.20).

Thus, the total short collection of stamp duty and registration fees due to
misclassification of conveyance deed as cancellation deed worked out to
= 36.90 lakh.

With respect to sale deeds, in one case, it was stated that document no.
9702/2022 was a settlement cancellation deed and hence no deficit stamp duty.
However, the document number corresponded to sale deed only. In another
case, it was stated that representation had been made by individual concerned
to District Registrar, which was pending. In all other cases of sale deed, it was

3.8.3 Non-recovery of concessional stamp duty
beneficiaries

ailed by woman

Failure to ensure recovery of the concessional stamp duty availed by
women beneficiaries, by violating the conditions resulted in
non-remittance of stamp duty of = 10.83 lakh.

The Government of Puducherry granted (December 2004) remission of
50 per cent of stamp duty to the women members acquiring property through
conveyance, exchange or gift either individually or severally. The remission
was subject to condition that the beneficiary shall not, within five years of
registration of the instrument through which the concession was obtained,
create/execute any instrument, including power of attorney, in favour of any
male member. In the event of violation, registering officer shall immediately
recover the amount at the time of execution of instrument of alienation in favour
of male member. Further, all the registering officers were instructed to
invariably affix a seal on the original deed, as well as on duplicate/certified
copies, wherever issued, indicating availment of the concession.

Scrutiny of records in six Sub-Registrar offices** revealed that 15 women
purchasers who had availed 50 per cent remission of stamp duty amounting to
₹ 10.83 lakh, sold their property to male members within five years of
registration of the instrument, as detailed in the Appendix 3.21. These women
purchasers had not remitted the concession availed, amounting to = 10.83 lakh.
Since the conditions subject to which the stamp duty concession was granted
were not adhered to, the registering officer should have recovered the amount
at the time of execution of instrument of alienation in favour of male member.
Failure by the registering officers to do so resulted in non-remittance of stamp
duty of = 10.83 lakh.

Thus, the failure to ensure recovery of the concessional stamp duty availed by
women beneficiaries, who violated the conditions resulted in non- remittance of
stamp duty amounting 10 = 10.83 lakh.

On being pointed out (July 2025) by audit, District Registrar replied
(October 2025) that in respect of four cases, हैं 2.29 lakh had already been
recovered and for other cases, notices had been/will be sent to individuals
concerned to remit the stamp duty. However, the reply of the Government is
still awaited.

CHAPTER IV: UNION TERRITORY PUBLIC SECTOR

ENTERPRISES

This Chapter includes the financial performance of Government Companies as
revealed from their accounts. The term Union Territory Public Sector
Enterprises (UTPSEs) encompasses those Government Companies in which the
direct shareholding of the Union Territory (UT) Government of Puducherry is
51 per cent or more and includes subsidiaries of such Government Companies.
Impact of significant comments issued as a result of supplementary audit of the
Financial Statements of these UTPSEs conducted by the Comptroller and
Auditor General (CAG) of India has also been discussed.

ment Comp:

A Government Company is defined in Section 2(45) of the Companies Act,
2013 as a company in which not less than 51 per cent of the paid-up share capital
is held by Central Government, or by any State Government or Governments,
or partly by the Central Government and partly by one or more State
Governments and includes a company which is a subsidiary of a Government
Company.

4.2

date

Audit of the Union Territory Government Companies is conducted by the CAG
under the provisions of Sections 143(5) to 143(7) of the Companies
Act (Act), 2013 read with Section 19 of the CAG’s (Duties, Powers and
Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 and the Regulations made thereunder. Under
the Act, the CAG appoints the Chartered Accountants as Statutory Auditors for
PSEs and gives directions on the manner in which the accounts are to be audited.
In addition, CAG has the right to conduct a supplementary audit of the
company’s financial statements and issue comments upon or supplement the
Audit Report of the Statutory Auditors. Further, CAG has also the power to
cause test audit of the accounts of such company to be conducted.

4.7.1 Interest Coverage Ratio

Interest coverage ratio (ICR) is used to determine the ability of a company to
pay interest on outstanding debt and is calculated by dividing a company's
earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT) by interest expenses of the same
period. The lower the ratio, the lesser is the ability of the company to pay
interest on debt. An ICR below one indicates that the company was not
generating sufficient revenues to meet its expenses on interest. The details of
positive and negative interest coverage ratio of UTPSEs, which had outstanding
loans during the period from 2021-22 to 2023-24 are given in Table 4.6.

4.8.1 Return on Capital Employed

Return on Capital Employed (RoCE) is a ratio that measures a company’s
profitability and the efficiency with which its capital is employed. RoCE is
calculated by dividing a company’s earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT)
by the capital employed". The details of RoCE during the period from

4.8.2 Return on Equity

Return on Equity (RoE)'"? is a measure of financial performance to assess how
effectively a company’s assets are being used to create profits. RoE is
calculated by dividing net income (i.e., net profit after taxes) by shareholders’
funds. It is expressed as a percentage and can be calculated for any company if
net income and shareholders’ funds are both positive numbers.

Shareholders’ funds are calculated by adding paid up capital and free reserves
minus accumulated losses and deferred revenue expenditure and reveals how
much would be left for a company’s shareholders, if all assets were sold and all
debts paid. A positive figure of shareholders’ fund reveals that the company
has enough assets to cover its liabilities while negative shareholders’ funds
mean liabilities exceed assets.

The details of shareholders’ fund and RoE of UTPSEs during the period from
2021-22 to 2023-24 are given in Table 4.8.

Capital Employed = Paid up Share capital + Free Reserves and surplus + Long term
loans -Accumulated losses - Deferred Revenue Expenditure.

Employed.

Expenditure.

4.11 Submission of accounts by UTP:

4.11.1 Need for timely submission

According to Sections 394 and 395 of the Companies Act, 2013, Annual Report
on the working and affairs of a Government Company is required to be prepared
within three months of its Annual General Meeting'’® (AGM). The Annual

The liabilities/establishment expenditure of the net worth eroded UTPSEs were met
from internal generation, budgetary support from UT Government, etc.

Represents Grants and Subsides in respect of five out of eight UTPSEs in respect of
which information was made available.

In case of the first AGM, it shall be held within a period of nine months from the date
of closing of the first financial year of the company and in any other case, within a
period of six months from the date of closing of the financial year i.e. 30 September
(Section 96 of the Companies Act, 2013).

120


Chapter IV - Union Territory Public Sector Enterprises

Report shall be laid before the UT Legislature together with a copy of the Audit
Report and comments of the CAG upon or as supplement to the Audit Report.
This mechanism seeks to provide the necessary legislative control over the
utilisation of public funds invested in the PSEs from the Consolidated Fund of
Union Territory.

Section 96 of the Companies Act, 2013 requires every company to hold AGM
of the shareholders once in every calendar year. It also prescribes that not more
than 15 months shall elapse between the date of one AGM and that of the next.
Further, Section 129 of the Companies Act, 2013 stipulates that the audited
Financial Statement for the financial year have to be placed in the said AGM
for their consideration.

Section 129(7) of the Companies Act, 2013 also provides for punishment like
fine and/or imprisonment on the persons including Directors of the Company
responsible for non-compliance with the provisions of Section 129 of the
Companies Act, 2013.

Despite the above, annual accounts of various UTPSEs were pending as detailed
in the following paragraph.

4.11.2 Timeliness in preparation of accounts by UTPSEs

As of 31 March 2024, there were 12 UTPSEs under the purview of CAG’s audit.
Of these, accounts for the year 2023-24 were due from all the
12 UTPSEs. Their accounts were in arrears ranging from one to 11 years for
various reasons (Appendix 4.3). During the year, six UTPSEs submitted their
10 arrear accounts for audit by CAG. Details of arrears in submission of
accounts by UTPSEs are given in Table 4.10.

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

The UT Government had made investment in the form of equity and loans in
the UTPSEs whose accounts had been falling in arrears and hence, the
non-finalisation of accounts by these UTPSEs would lead to the Government
investments remaining outside the oversight of Audit as well as the UT
Legislature. The subject of clearance of arrears in finalisation of accounts is
being brought to the attention of the Chief Secretary, UT Government
periodically by the PAG. The UT Government convened meeting on
03 February 2025 and fixed 31 March 2025 as an outer timeline to complete the
backlog in clearance of arrears in finalisation of accounts. However, since the
number of arrear accounts has been increasing over the years, the issue needs
serious intervention at the highest level of governance.

audit

4.12.1 Financial reporting framework

Companies are required to prepare the Financial Statements in the format laid
down in Schedule III to the Companies Act, 2013 and in adherence to the
mandatory Accounting Standards prescribed by the Central Government, in
consultation with the National Financial Reporting Authority on Accounting
Standards.

4.12.2 Audit of accounts of UTPSEs by Statutory Auditors and
Supplementary Audit by CAG

The primary responsibility for preparation of financial statements in accordance

with the financial reporting framework prescribed under the Companies Act,

2013 is that of the management of an entity.

The statutory auditors appointed by the CAG under Section 139 of the
Companies Act, 2013 conduct audit of accounts of the UTPSEs. They are
responsible for expressing an opinion on the financial statements under Section
143 of the Companies Act, 2013, based on an independent audit in accordance
with the Standard Auditing Practices of Institute of Chartered Accountants of
India (ICAI) and directions given by the CAG. The Statutory Auditors are
required to submit the Audit Report to the CAG under Section 143 of the
Companies Act, 2013.

The CAG plays an oversight role by monitoring the performance of the statutory
auditors in audit of PSEs with the overall objective that the statutory auditors
discharge the functions assigned to them properly and effectively by:

> issuing directions to the statutory auditors under Section 143(5) of the
Companies Act, 2013; and

122


Chapter IV - Union Territory Public Sector Enterprises

> supplement or comment upon the statutory auditor's report under
Section 143(6) of the Companies Act, 2013.

The certified accounts of selected UTPSEs along with the report of the statutory
auditors are reviewed by CAG by carrying out a supplementary audit. Based
on such reviews, significant audit observations, if any, are reported under
Section 143(6) of the Companies Act, 2013 to be placed before the Annual
General Meeting.

Result of CAG

None of the 12 Government Companies submitted their Financial Statements
for the year 2023-24. As these companies were in arrears in submission of their
financial statements, they did not comply with the provisions of Section 96 and
Section 129 of the Companies Act, 2013 with respect to the provisions relating
to holding of the AGM. However, six'!? UTPSEs submitted their
10 arrear accounts during the year 2023-24. Of these, three accounts'* of three
UTPSEs were reviewed in audit by the CAG and non-review certificates were
issued for the seven accounts of four UTPSEs. The results of the review are
detailed below:

4.13.1 Amendment of Financial Statements

During 2023-24, none of the three UTPSEs which were taken up for
supplementary audit, amended their financial statements before laying the same
in the AGM.

4.13.2 Revision of Auditors’ Report

The statutory auditors’ report on the Financial Statements of one'? UTPSE was
revised as a result of the supplementary audit conducted by the CAG.

(2019-20 and 2020-21), Puducherry Distilleries Corporation Limited (2017-18,

Audit Report for the period ended March 2024

4.14 Management Letters

One of the objectives of financial audit is to establish communication on audit
matters arising from the audit of Financial Statements between the auditor and
those charged with the responsibility of governance of the corporate entity.

The material observations on the Financial Statements of Public Sector
Enterprises are reported as comments by the CAG under Section 143(5) of the
Companies Act, 2013. Besides these comments, irregularities or deficiencies
observed by CAG in the financial reports or in the reporting process, were also
communicated to the Management through ‘Management Letter’ for taking
corrective action. During the year, Management Letters were issued to three”?
UTPSEs, in respect of the deficiencies related to accounting treatment and
policies.

4.15 Conclusion

As on March 2024, there were 12 UTPSEs (excluding one non-functional
UTPSE). None of the 12 UTPSEs adhered to the prescribed timeline regarding
submission of their Financial Statements. There were 62 accounts of
12 UTPSEs in arrears ranging from one to 11 years.

Out of the total profit of = 49.45 crore earned by five UTPSEs as per the latest
available finalised accounts, 94.64 per cent was contributed by three UTPSEs.
The remaining seven UTPSEs incurred a total loss of = 46.67 crore leading to
an overall loss of = 2.78 crore. Two out of three UTPSEs which finalised their
arrear accounts during 2023-24 declared a dividend of % 7.40 crore. The Net
worth of eight UTPSEs was negative at = 339.63 crore as their equity had been
completely eroded by accumulated losses.

4.16 ommendations

e UT Government may either reconsider continuance of those
Government Companies which have been loss making for very long and
whose net worth has been eroded, especially if these companies are not
fulfilling any social welfare purpose or initiate efficient measures to
make their operations efficient and profitable.

. UT Government may ensure strict compliance by UTPSEs to adhere to

the timeline fixed by it for clearance of arrear accounts.

Puducherry Adi-Dravidar and Scheduled Tribes Development Corporation Limited
(2020-21), Puducherry Distilleries Corporation Limited (2020-21) and Puducherry
Power Corporation Limited (2021-22).

124


Chapter IV - Union Territory Public Sector Enterprises

UT Government may ensure timely submission of Financial Statements
of UTPSEs, as in the absence of finalisation of accounts, Government
investments in such UTPSEs remain outside the oversight of the UT

Legislature.
